# Sources

> Every source cited in the knowledge files, by ID. Each one was opened (abstract or full text) during research and again by an independent checker; `Opened` is the URL that actually returned the text. All accessed 2026-10-24. Sources that could not be opened were dropped together with their claims.

Fields: type, DOI, canonical URL, opened URL, which files cite it, and what in the opened text supports the claims that cite it.

## Summary

| Type | Count |
|---|---|
| Meta-analysis | 296 |
| Other | 112 |
| RCT | 96 |
| Cohort | 86 |
| Systematic review | 91 |
| Guideline | 78 |
| Narrative review | 48 |
| Position stand | 37 |
| Umbrella review | 15 |
| **Total** | **859** |

## List

### S001
Currier BS et al., 2026. ACSM Position Stand: Resistance Training Prescription for Muscle Function, Hypertrophy, and Physical Performance in Healthy Adults: An Overview of Reviews. Med Sci Sports Exerc 58(4):851-872  
Type: Position stand · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41843416/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=PMC12965823&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Abstract: 137 systematic reviews; 'Muscle hypertrophy was enhanced by higher volumes (>=10 sets/wk)'. Text: 'higher volume (>=10 sets/muscle group/wk)'. Table 6: 'Volume: >=10 sets/wk (dose-response)'.

### S002
Schoenfeld BJ, Ogborn D, Krieger JW, 2017. Dose-response relationship between weekly resistance training volume and increases in muscle mass: a systematic review and meta-analysis. J Sports Sci 35(11):1073-1082  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1080/02640414.2016.1210197](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2016.1210197) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27433992/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=27433992>  
Supports: 34 groups, 15 studies. Each additional set: ES +0.023, +0.37% gain (P=0.002). Higher vs lower: ES difference 0.241, 3.9% (P=0.03). 3-level categories: P=0.074.

### S003
Pelland JC et al., 2026. The Resistance Training Dose Response: Meta-Regressions Exploring the Effects of Weekly Volume and Frequency on Muscle Hypertrophy and Strength Gains. Sports Med 56(2):481-505  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-025-02344-w](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-025-02344-w) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41343037/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=41343037>  
Supports: 67 studies, 2058 participants (79.1% male, mean age 25.16). Posterior probability of a positive volume slope was 100% for hypertrophy and strength, with diminishing returns.

### S004
Krieger JW, 2010. Single vs. multiple sets of resistance exercise for muscle hypertrophy: a meta-analysis. J Strength Cond Res 24(4):1150-1159  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181d4d436](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181d4d436) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20300012/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=20300012>  
Supports: 55 ESs, 19 groups, 8 studies. Multiple vs single ES difference 0.10 (p=0.016). ES 0.24 (1 set), 0.34 (2-3), 0.44 (4-6). '40% greater hypertrophy-related ESs than 1 set, in both trained and untrained'. 2-3 vs 4-6 sets p=0.29.

### S005
Currier BS et al., 2023. Resistance training prescription for muscle strength and hypertrophy in healthy adults: a systematic review and Bayesian network meta-analysis. Br J Sports Med 57(18):1211-1220  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2023-106807](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2023-106807) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37414459/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=37414459>  
Supports: Hypertrophy network: 119 studies, n=3364, 47% women. Higher-load multiset twice-weekly ranked highest (SMD 0.66, 95% CrI 0.47-0.85). 'all prescriptions comparably promoted muscle hypertrophy'.

### S006
Schoenfeld BJ et al., 2019. Resistance Training Volume Enhances Muscle Hypertrophy but Not Strength in Trained Men. Med Sci Sports Exerc 51(1):94-103  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000001764](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000001764) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30153194/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=30153194>  
Supports: n=34 trained men; 1, 3 or 5 sets per exercise, 3 sessions/week, 8 weeks. No between-group strength or endurance differences. Hypertrophy favoured higher volume at the elbow flexors, mid-thigh and lateral thigh.

### S007
Baz-Valle E et al., 2022. A Systematic Review of The Effects of Different Resistance Training Volumes on Muscle Hypertrophy. J Hum Kinet 81:199-210  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.2478/hukin-2022-0017](https://doi.org/10.2478/hukin-2022-0017) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35291645/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=35291645>  
Supports: Categories <12, 12-20, >20 sets. Moderate vs high: quadriceps p=0.19, biceps p=0.59, triceps p=0.01 in favour of high. '12-20 weekly sets per muscle group may be an optimum standard recommendation ... in young, trained men'.

### S008
Ralston GW et al., 2017. The Effect of Weekly Set Volume on Strength Gain: A Meta-Analysis. Sports Med 47(12):2585-2601  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-017-0762-7](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-017-0762-7) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28755103/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=28755103>  
Supports: High vs low weekly sets: ES 0.18 (0.06-0.30), p=0.003. Medium vs low: ES 0.15 (0.01-0.30). Graded dose-response.

### S009
Androulakis-Korakakis P, Fisher JP, Steele J, 2020. The Minimum Effective Training Dose Required to Increase 1RM Strength in Resistance-Trained Men: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. Sports Med 50(4):751-765  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-019-01236-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-019-01236-0) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31797219/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=31797219&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 6 studies included, 5 meta-analysed. Overall 1RM +12.09 kg [8.16-16.03], SQ +17.48 kg [8.51-26.46], BP +8.25 kg [0.68-15.83]. Single set of 6-12 reps, ~70-85% 1RM, 2-3x/week, failure, 8-12 weeks: 'suboptimal, yet significant'.

### S010
Iversen VM, Norum M, Schoenfeld BJ, Fimland MS, 2021. No Time to Lift? Designing Time-Efficient Training Programs for Strength and Hypertrophy: A Narrative Review. Sports Med 51(10):2079-2095  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-021-01490-1](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-021-01490-1) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34125411/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=34125411&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 'Weekly training volume is more important than training frequency and we recommend performing a minimum of 4 weekly sets per muscle group using a 6-15 RM loading range (15-40 repetitions can be used if training is performed to volitional failure)'. Supersets etc. 'roughly halves training time'.

### S011
Schoenfeld BJ, Grgic J, Krieger J, 2019. How many times per week should a muscle be trained to maximize muscle hypertrophy? A systematic review and meta-analysis. J Sports Sci 37(11):1286-1295  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1080/02640414.2018.1555906](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2018.1555906) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30558493/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=30558493&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 25 studies. No significant difference on a volume-equated basis. 'strong evidence that resistance training frequency does not significantly or meaningfully impact muscle hypertrophy when volume is equated'.

### S012
Grgic J et al., 2018. Effect of Resistance Training Frequency on Gains in Muscular Strength: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. Sports Med 48(5):1207-1220  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-018-0872-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-018-0872-x) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29470825/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=29470825&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: ES 0.74/0.82/0.93/1.08 for 1/2/3/4+ sessions/week (p=0.003). Volume-equated subgroup showed no significant effect. Effect seen in young adults (p=0.024) and women (p=0.030).

### S013
Ramos-Campo DJ et al., 2024. Efficacy of Split Versus Full-Body Resistance Training on Strength and Muscle Growth: A Systematic Review With Meta-Analysis. J Strength Cond Res 38(7):1330-1340  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1519/JSC.0000000000004774](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0000000000004774) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38595233/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=38595233&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 14 studies, n=392. Bench MD 1.19 [-1.28, 3.65]. Lower limb MD 2.47 [-2.11, 7.05]. Elbow extensors MD 0.30 [-2.65, 3.24]. Elbow flexors MD 0.17 [-2.54, 2.88]. VL MD -0.08 [-1.82, 1.66]. Lean mass MD -0.07 [-1.59, 1.44]. No significant impact 'when volume is equated'.

### S014
Evangelista AL et al., 2021. Split or full-body workout routine: which is best to increase muscle strength and hypertrophy? Einstein (Sao Paulo) 19:eAO5781  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.31744/einstein_journal/2021AO5781](https://doi.org/10.31744/einstein_journal/2021AO5781) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34468591/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=34468591&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 67 untrained subjects, 8 weeks. 2x vs 4x/week per muscle with equal weekly sets gave no between-group differences in strength or muscle thickness.

### S015
Carneiro MAS et al., 2024. Full-body resistance training promotes greater fat mass loss than a split-body routine in well-trained males: A randomized trial. Eur J Sport Sci 24(6):846-854  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1002/ejsc.12104](https://doi.org/10.1002/ejsc.12104) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38874955/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=38874955&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=23 well-trained males, 75 sets/week matched, 8 weeks. Full-body: whole-body fat -0.775 kg. Split: +0.317 kg (p=0.040).

### S016
Bull FC et al., 2020. World Health Organization 2020 guidelines on physical activity and sedentary behaviour. Br J Sports Med 54(24):1451-1462  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2020-102955](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2020-102955) · Files: strength-hypertrophy, cardio-health, recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33239350/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=PMC7719906&rettype=xml>  
Supports: 'Adults should also do muscle-strengthening activities at moderate or greater intensity that involve all major muscle groups on 2 or more days a week ... Strong recommendation'. 'a strong recommendation supported by moderate-certainty evidence. There was no evidence to support a dose-response association with higher volumes of muscle-strengthening activities.' Older adults: 'As for adults, plus ... varied multicomponent physical activity that emphasises functional balance and strength training at moderate or greater intensity on 3 or more days a week ... Strong recommendation'.

### S017
Borde R, Hortobagyi T, Granacher U, 2015. Dose-Response Relationships of Resistance Training in Healthy Old Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. Sports Med 45(12):1693-1720  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-015-0385-9](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-015-0385-9) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26420238/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26420238&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Strength SMD 1.57 (25 studies), morphology 0.42 (9 studies). Strength: 2 sessions/week SMD 2.13, 2-3 sets SMD 2.99, 7-9 reps SMD 1.98. Morphology: 'a training frequency of three sessions per week, a training volume of two to three sets per exercise, seven to nine repetitions per set ... most effective'. PEDro 4.6, I2=80%; interactions could not be determined.

### S018
Kneffel Z, Murlasits Z, Reed J, Krieger J, 2021. A meta-regression of the effects of resistance training frequency on muscular strength and hypertrophy in adults over 60 years of age. J Sports Sci 39(3):351-358  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1080/02640414.2020.1822595](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2020.1822595) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32948100/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=32948100&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 14 articles; ES = pre-post change / pooled pre SD. Strength +0.14 ES per extra day (CI 0.08-0.21), p=0.001. Hypertrophy p=0.67. 'unlikely that more than two weekly resistance training sessions would provide any further benefits for older adults'.

### S019
Hammarstrom D et al., 2020. Benefits of higher resistance-training volume are related to ribosome biogenesis. J Physiol 598(3):543-565  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1113/JP278455](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP278455) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31813190/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=31813190&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 3 sets vs 1 set: CSA 5.2% vs 3.7% (p<0.001). 'Thirteen and sixteen participants, respectively, displayed clear benefits in response to MOD on muscle hypertrophy and strength'.

### S020
Remmert JF, Pelland JC, Robinson ZP, Hinson SR, Zourdos MC, 2025. Is There Too Much of a Good Thing? Meta-Regressions of the Effect of Per-Session Volume on Hypertrophy and Strength. SportRxiv preprint  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.51224/SRXIV.537](https://doi.org/10.51224/SRXIV.537) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/view/537>  
Supports: 'the PUOS occurring at ~2 direct sets for strength and ~11 fractional sets for hypertrophy' ... 'there is insufficient data with very high per-session set volumes ... the PUOS should be interpreted cautiously.'

### S021
Lopez P et al., 2021. Resistance Training Load Effects on Muscle Hypertrophy and Strength Gain: Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis. Med Sci Sports Exerc 53(6):1206-1216  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000002585](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000002585) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33433148/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=33433148&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 28 studies, n=747, all sets to volitional failure: no difference in hypertrophy between low, moderate and high loads (P=0.113-0.469); 'muscle hypertrophy improvements seem to be load independent'

### S022
Schoenfeld BJ et al., 2017. Strength and Hypertrophy Adaptations Between Low- vs. High-Load Resistance Training: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis. J Strength Cond Res 31(12):3508-3523  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1519/JSC.0000000000002200](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0000000000002200) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28834797/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=28834797&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 21 studies, low load <=60% 1RM vs high >60% 1RM, all sets to momentary failure: 'Changes in measures of muscle hypertrophy were similar between conditions'

### S023
Csapo R, Alegre LM, 2016. Effects of resistance training with moderate vs heavy loads on muscle mass and strength in the elderly: A meta-analysis. Scand J Med Sci Sports 26(9):995-1006  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1111/sms.12536](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12536) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26302881/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26302881&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 15 studies, 448 older adults (mean 67.8 y): heavy (~80% 1RM) loads 'provoked marginally larger gains in muscle size', hypertrophy generally small (0.056 < mu < 0.136)

### S024
Grgic J et al., 2022. Effects of resistance training performed to repetition failure or non-failure on muscular strength and hypertrophy: A systematic review and meta-analysis. J Sport Health Sci 11(2):202-211  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/j.jshs.2021.01.007](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jshs.2021.01.007) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33497853/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=33497853&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 15 studies, all young adults; strength ES -0.09 (-0.22 to 0.05); hypertrophy ES 0.22 (-0.11 to 0.55); non-volume-equated favoured non-failure for strength (ES -0.32); trained subgroup hypertrophy ES 0.15 (0.03-0.26)

### S025
Refalo MC et al., 2023. Influence of Resistance Training Proximity-to-Failure on Skeletal Muscle Hypertrophy: A Systematic Review with Meta-analysis. Sports Med 53(3):649-665  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-022-01784-y](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01784-y) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36334240/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=36334240&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 15 studies; momentary failure vs non-failure ES 0.12 (-0.13 to 0.37), p=0.343; any set-failure definition ES 0.19 (0.00 to 0.37), 'trivial advantage'

### S026
Wu S et al., 2026. Effects of resistance training performed to repetition non-failure on exercise performance in healthy adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis. BMC Sports Sci Med Rehabil 18(1):333  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s13102-026-01861-z](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13102-026-01861-z) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42410632/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=42410632&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 20 studies (RCTs), 556 participants: non-failure superior for dynamic strength (SMD 0.24, 0.06-0.42); no significant effects for isometric strength, hypertrophy, endurance, power

### S027
Robinson ZP et al., 2024. Exploring the Dose-Response Relationship Between Estimated Resistance Training Proximity to Failure, Strength Gain, and Muscle Hypertrophy: A Series of Meta-Regressions. Sports Med 54(9):2209-2231  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-024-02069-2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-024-02069-2) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38970765/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=38970765&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Hypertrophy slopes for estimated RIR negative with CIs excluding null; strength slopes CIs contained null; 'caution is warranted ... exploratory nature'

### S028
Morán-Navarro R et al., 2017. Time course of recovery following resistance training leading or not to failure. Eur J Appl Physiol 117(12):2387-2399  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1007/s00421-017-3725-7](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-017-3725-7) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28965198/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=28965198&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Ten resistance-trained men, BP and SQ; 3x10(10) vs 6x5(10): higher acute decline of CMJ and velocity; recovery from non-failure protocols 'significantly faster between 24 and 48 h'; failure 'slows down recovery up to 24-48 h post-exercise'

### S029
Refalo MC et al., 2025. The Effect of Proximity-To-Failure on Perceptual Responses to Resistance Training. Eur J Sport Sci 25(3):e12266  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1002/ejsc.12266](https://doi.org/10.1002/ejsc.12266) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39960821/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=39960821&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 12 M + 6 F trained, 8 weeks; RPD FAIL 5.1 vs RIR 4.1; session RPE FAIL 5.4 vs RIR 4.3; feeling scale RIR 1.2 vs FAIL 0.3

### S030
Refalo MC et al., 2024. Similar muscle hypertrophy following eight weeks of resistance training to momentary muscular failure or with repetitions-in-reserve in resistance-trained individuals. J Sports Sci 42(1):85-101  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1080/02640414.2024.2321021](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2024.2321021) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38393985/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=38393985&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=18 trained; quadriceps thickness +0.181 cm FAIL vs +0.182 cm RIR; velocity and repetition loss consistently greater with FAIL; improved fatigue resistance over time may attenuate acute fatigue

### S031
Halperin I et al., 2022. Accuracy in Predicting Repetitions to Task Failure in Resistance Exercise: A Scoping Review and Exploratory Meta-analysis. Sports Med 52(2):377-390  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-021-01559-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-021-01559-x) · Files: strength-hypertrophy, recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34542869/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=34542869&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 12 studies, 414 participants; underprediction 0.95 reps (0.17-1.73), I2=97.9%; ≤12 reps β 0.06 vs >12 reps β 0.47; later sets β -0.07; training status β -0.006; between-participant SD 1.45 reps

### S032
Remmert JF, Laurson KR, Zourdos MC, 2023. Accuracy of Predicted Intraset Repetitions in Reserve (RIR) in Single- and Multi-Joint Resistance Exercises Among Trained and Untrained Men and Women. Percept Mot Skills 130(3):1239-1254  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.1177/00315125231169868](https://doi.org/10.1177/00315125231169868) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37036795/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=37036795&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 27 men, 31 women; sets to failure at 72.5% 1RM; more accurate closer to failure and in later sets (p<0.01); sex (p=0.917) and training experience (p=0.462) had no effect

### S033

### S038
Grgic J, Lazinica B, Mikulic P, Krieger JW, Schoenfeld BJ, 2017. The effects of short versus long inter-set rest intervals in resistance training on measures of muscle hypertrophy: A systematic review. Eur J Sport Sci 17(8):983-993

Supports: 86 studies, n=5,670; body mass, waist circumference and VO2max remained improved in short-, medium- and long-term subgroups; SBP in short and long term; 'muscle mass, lean body mass ... were no longer significant in any of the follow-up subgroups'.

### S067
Halonen EJ, Gabriel I, Kelahaara MM, Ahtiainen JP, Hulmi JJ, 2024. Does Taking a Break Matter-Adaptations in Muscle Strength and Size Between Continuous and Periodic Resistance Training. Scand J Med Sci Sports 34(10):e14739

### S068

Sec 4.4: volume-hypertrophy fractional vs total 9.48 (strong), vs direct 10.29 (very strong); volume-strength 18.21 and 45.96. Discussion: 'suffers from the assumption that all indirect training should be quantified as half a set'.

### S073
Schoenfeld BJ, Grgic J, Haun C, Itagaki T, Helms ER. 2019. Calculating Set-Volume for the Limb Muscles with the Performance of Multi-Joint Exercises: Implications for Resistance Training Prescription. Sports (Basel) 7(7):177.  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.3390/sports7070177](https://doi.org/10.3390/sports7070177) · Files: strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31336594/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=6681288&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Abstract: 'sets for a MJ exercise should be counted fractionally for select muscles'; hamstring activation 'markedly lower than that of the quadriceps ... a 1:1 ratio cannot be assumed'; longitudinal evidence 'limited to the elbow flexors and ... somewhat conflicting'; 'we propose the best advice would be to view set-volume prescription on a 1:1 basis'. Full text: hamstring EMG '~twofold lower' than quadriceps in squat and leg press; rectus femoris 68% MVC in leg extension vs 39% in leg press and 27% in squat; pec major ~twofold greater EMG than triceps in bench press; 'likely depends on the individual and the technical execution'.

Supports: 8 studies. Harris-Benedict had the smallest differences between bias at 3, 6 and 12 months (6 mo MD 59.16 kcal), but 'large estimated predictive intervals may indicate that predictive equations may not be handy tools' for individuals.

### S164
Piassa TR et al., 2026, To measure or to estimate? A comparison between indirect calorimetry and predictive equations for resting metabolic rate in institutionalized older adults: a systematic review, Arch Gerontol Geriatr  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1016/j.archger.2026.106408](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.archger.2026.106408) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42759166/>

Supports: 4 studies, n=332. 'Most of the analyzed equations (79%) showed a tendency to overestimate RMR'; 'Individual accuracy was considered low, not exceeding 41%'. Lührmann acceptable; Harris-Benedict and FAO/WHO/UNU low accuracy.

### S165
O'Neill JER et al., 2023, Accuracy of Resting Metabolic Rate Prediction Equations in Athletes: A Systematic Review with Meta-analysis, Sports Med  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-023-01896-z](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-023-01896-z) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37632665/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=21872751,23774459,15883556,38548479,38057015,42759166,37632665,24222017&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 29 studies, 1430 participants (5 studies of recreational exercisers). Ten-Haaf predicted '80.2% of participants to be within ± 10%', others 40.7-63.7%. Mifflin-St Jeor, Owen, FAO/WHO/UNU significantly under/overestimated. I² 80-93% for the others vs I²=0% for Ten-Haaf. 'no single equation is guaranteed to be superior'.

### S166
FAO/WHO/UNU, 2004, Human energy requirements: Report of a Joint FAO/WHO/UNU Expert Consultation (Food and Nutrition Technical Report Series 1), chapter 5 (adults)  
Type: Guideline · DOI: none · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://www.fao.org/4/y5686e/y5686e07.htm>  
Supports: Table 5.3: sedentary/light 1.40-1.69, active/moderately active 1.70-1.99, vigorous 2.00-2.40; 'PAL values > 2.40 are difficult to maintain over a long period of time.' 'PAL = TEE/BMR. Multiplying the PAL by the BMR gives the actual energy requirements.' Modal PAL 1.60 (1.55-1.65) in sedentary Western adults; one hour daily of moderate-vigorous exercise 'can raise a person's average PAL from 1.55' to about 1.75.

Rec 2: overweight/obese adults with CV risk factors, 'sustained weight loss of 3%-5% produce clinically meaningful health benefits' A (Strong). 'The Expert Panel recommends as an initial goal the loss of 5%-10% of baseline weight within 6 months.'

Supports: 21 randomized, 17 completers (2 female); ≥1 y RT required. Intake change: MOD +489±184, HIGH +719±189, MAIN +169 kcal/d. Body mass change: MOD 3.3±0.8, HIGH 3.3±1.5, MAIN 0.4±0.5 kg. BM change predicts ST change (BF10=14.3, R2=0.49), weak evidence for biceps MT (R2=0.24). HIGH increased bench 1-RM more. The authors note a prescribed 5% surplus 'in practice becomes a larger surplus' and recommend '5-20% over maintenance ... or 0.25-0.5% of body mass per week, scaled to RT experience'.

### S197
Trommelen J et al., 2023. The anabolic response to protein ingestion during recovery from exercise has no upper limit in magnitude and duration in vivo in humans. Cell Rep Med 4(12):101324.

### S198
Schoenfeld BJ, Aragon AA, Krieger JW, 2013. The effect of protein timing on muscle strength and hypertrophy: a meta-analysis. J Int Soc Sports Nutr 10(1):53.

EFSA NDA Panel, 2012. Scientific Opinion on Dietary Reference Values for protein. EFSA Journal 10(2):2557 (text read from the 2011 public-consultation draft).

### S200
Devries MC et al., 2018. Changes in kidney function do not differ between healthy adults consuming higher- compared with lower- or normal-protein diets: a systematic review and meta-analysis. J Nutr 148(11):1760-1775.

Supports: 19 studies. Fat-free mass MD -0.24 kg (-1.15 to 0.67), described as 'preserving fat-free mass'.

### S233
He M et al., 2024. Does early time-restricted eating reduce body weight and preserve fat-free mass in adults? A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. Diabetes Metab Syndr 18(2):102952

### S234
Hays HM et al., 2025. Effects of time-restricted eating with exercise on body composition in adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Int J Obes 49(5):755-765

### S235
Ali AM et al., 2026. Time-restricted eating shows a modest reduction in fat mass in resistance-trained individuals: A systematic review and meta-analysis. Nutr Res 147:1-15

### S236
Koch CA, Kjeldsen EW, Frikke-Schmidt R, 2023. Vegetarian or vegan diets and blood lipids: a meta-analysis of randomized trials. Eur Heart J 44(28):2609-2622

Melina V, Craig W, Levin S, 2016. Position of the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics: Vegetarian Diets. J Acad Nutr Diet 116(12):1970-1980

### S238
Dinu M et al., 2017. Vegetarian, vegan diets and multiple health outcomes: A systematic review with meta-analysis of observational studies. Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 57(17):3640-3649

Supports: 86 cross-sectional and 10 prospective cohort studies. IHD RR 0.75 (0.68-0.82), total cancer incidence RR 0.92 (0.87-0.98). No association with total cardio/cerebrovascular disease, all-cause mortality or cancer mortality.

### S239
Tong TYN et al., 2019. Risks of ischaemic heart disease and stroke in meat eaters, fish eaters, and vegetarians over 18 years of follow-up: results from the prospective EPIC-Oxford study. BMJ 366:l4897  
Type: Cohort · DOI: [10.1136/bmj.l4897](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.l4897) · Files: nutrition

Supports: 48,188 people, 18.1 years. Vegetarians (including vegans): IHD HR 0.78 (0.70-0.87), 10 fewer cases per 1,000 over 10 years. HR 0.90 (0.81-1.00) after full adjustment. Total stroke HR 1.20 (1.02-1.40), 3 more per 1,000 over 10 years, mostly haemorrhagic, not attenuated.

Niklewicz A et al., 2024. A systematic review and meta-analysis of functional vitamin B12 status among adult vegans. Nutr Bull

### S241
Hevia-Larraín V et al., 2021. High-Protein Plant-Based Diet Versus a Protein-Matched Omnivorous Diet to Support Resistance Training Adaptations: A Comparison Between Habitual Vegans and Omnivores. Sports Med 51(6):1317-1330

Full text (PMC7871530): early loading water gain short term, creatine 'may not lead to water retention'; no study reports hair loss in humans; no support for dehydration or cramping; 3-5 g/day for at least 4 weeks gives similar saturation to loading; loading typically adds 1-3 kg, mostly water.

### S245
Liu S, Huang N, Wu W, et al., 2025, The impact of creatine supplementation associated with resistance training on muscular strength and lean tissue mass in the aged: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Eur Rev Aging Phys Act 22(1):26

### S251
Comité Científico AESAN (López García E, coord., et al.), 2022, Informe del Comité Científico de la AESAN sobre recomendaciones dietéticas sostenibles y recomendaciones de actividad física para la población española (AESAN-2022-007). Revista del Comité Científico de la AESAN 36:11-70

### S326
Kerksick CM et al. 2018. ISSN exercise & sports nutrition review update: research & recommendations. J Int Soc Sports Nutr 15:38.

URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30068354/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=6090881&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Full text: people in a general fitness programme 'can typically meet daily carbohydrate needs by consuming a normal diet (i.e., 45-55% CHO [3-5 g/kg/day]...)'. Moderate intense training (2-3 h/day, 5-6 times/week) needs 5-8 g/kg/day. High-volume training (3-6 h/day) needs '8-10 g/day', a typo for g/kg/day. The authors state the paper is the official ISSN position. Table 3 sorts supplements into 'I. Strong Evidence to Support Efficacy and Apparently Safe', 'II. Limited or Mixed Evidence to Support Efficacy' and 'III. Little to No Evidence to Support Efficacy and/or Safety'; 'we suggest that they generally only recommend supplements in category I'.

### S327
Henselmans M, Bjørnsen T, Hedderman R, Vårvik FT. 2022. The Effect of Carbohydrate Intake on Strength and Resistance Training Performance: A Systematic Review. Nutrients 14(4):856.

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=35215506>  
Supports: Abstract: 49 studies. Acutely, carbohydrate 'did not improve performance in 13 studies and enhanced performance in six', mainly with fasted controls and more than 10 sets per muscle group. 'Longer-term changes in performance were not influenced by carbohydrate intake in 15 studies'. 'Carbohydrate intake per se is unlikely to [enhance] strength training performance in a fed state in workouts consisting of up to 10 sets per muscle group.'

Supports: Abstract: 11 trials, 888 participants, low-fat defined as ≤30% of energy. 'No significant differences' in estradiol, estrone, SHBG, DHEA, testosterone (95% CI -14.48, 13.50), progesterone or androstenedione. 'Tempered by the small number of studies and low certainty of evidence.'

### S334
Wu AH, Pike MC, Stram DO. 1999. Meta-analysis: dietary fat intake, serum estrogen levels, and the risk of breast cancer. J Natl Cancer Inst 91(6):529-534.  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1093/jnci/91.6.529](https://doi.org/10.1093/jnci/91.6.529) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10088623/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=10088623>  
Supports: Abstract: 13 studies. Estradiol -7.4% (95% CI -11.7 to -2.9) premenopausal and -23.0% (-27.7 to -18.1) postmenopausal. 'The greatest reductions occurred in two studies in which dietary fat was reduced to 10%-12% of calories compared with 18%-25%' in the others. The -6.6% reduction stayed significant after excluding those two studies.

### S335
Reichman ME et al. 1992. Effect of dietary fat on length of the follicular phase of the menstrual cycle in a controlled diet setting. J Clin Endocrinol Metab 74(5):1171-1175.  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.1210/jcem.74.5.1569164](https://doi.org/10.1210/jcem.74.5.1569164) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1569164/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=1569164>  
Supports: Abstract: 30 premenopausal women on weight-maintaining controlled diets, 4 cycles at 40% fat followed by 4 cycles at 20% fat (fixed order; groups differed only by P/S ratio). The follicular phase increased significantly on low fat (P<0.006). 'Two thirds of the women showed increases in follicular phase length with an average increase of 1.9 days.'

### S336
Mountjoy M et al. 2023. 2023 International Olympic Committee's (IOC) consensus statement on Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport (REDs). Br J Sports Med 57(17):1073-1097.  
Type: Position stand · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2023-106994) · Files: nutrition, recovery-habits

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=37752011>  
Supports: Abstract: REDs is 'a syndrome of deleterious health and performance outcomes experienced by female and male athletes exposed to low energy availability (LEA; inadequate energy intake in relation to exercise energy expenditure)'. It cites 'emerging data demonstrating the growing role of low carbohydrate availability' and a Physiological Model of 'LEA exposure, coupled with individual moderating factors'.

### S337
Lieberman JL, De Souza MJ, Wagstaff DA, Williams NI. 2018. Menstrual Disruption with Exercise Is Not Linked to an Energy Availability Threshold. Med Sci Sports Exerc 50(3):551-561.  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000001451](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000001451) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29023359/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=29023359>  
Supports: Abstract: 35 sedentary ovulatory women aged 18-24, randomised to different energy deficits; in a secondary analysis EA was calculated from energy-balance data. 'Likelihood of a MD decreased by 9% for each unit increase in EA (odds ratio, 0.91; 95% CI 0.84-0.98)'. 'No specific value of EA emerged as a threshold'. EA tertiles ranged from 23.4 to 50.1 kcal/kg FFM/d. 'MD increase linearly as EA decreases.'

### S338
World Health Organization. 2015. Guideline: Sugars intake for adults and children. Geneva: WHO (and WHO news release 4 March 2015).  
Type: Guideline · DOI: none · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789241549028>  
Opened: <https://www.who.int/news/item/04-03-2015-who-calls-on-countries-to-reduce-sugars-intake-among-adults-and-children>  
Supports: News release: free sugars 'less than 10% of their total energy intake' ('strong' recommendation). 'Further reduction to below 5% or roughly 25 grams (6 teaspoons) per day' is 'conditional', given few studies, mainly three ecological dental-caries studies. The free-sugars definition covers honey, syrups, fruit juices and concentrates. The guideline does not refer to sugars in fresh fruit and vegetables or sugars naturally present in milk.

### S339
EFSA NDA Panel (Turck D et al.). 2022. Tolerable upper intake level for dietary sugars. EFSA Journal 20(2):e07074.  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.2903/j.efsa.2022.7074](https://doi.org/10.2903/j.efsa.2022.7074) · Files: nutrition

Supports: Abstract: cohorts 'do not support a positive relationship... in isocaloric exchange'. RCTs: certainty 'moderate for obesity and dyslipidaemia (> 50-75% probability), low for non-alcoholic fatty liver disease and type 2 diabetes (> 15-50%)... very low for hypertension (0-15%)'. 'A UL or a safe level of intake could not be set'. Intake 'should be as low as possible in the context of a nutritionally adequate diet'.

AESAN Scientific Committee. 2022. Informe del Comité Científico de la AESAN sobre recomendaciones dietéticas sostenibles y recomendaciones de actividad física para la población española (AESAN-2022-007). Revista del Comité Científico de la AESAN.

### S377
Sawka MN et al., American College of Sports Medicine, 2007. ACSM position stand: Exercise and fluid replacement. Med Sci Sports Exerc 39(2):377-90

Drinking aims 'to prevent excessive (>2% body weight loss from water deficit) dehydration'. It notes 'considerable variability in sweating rates and sweat electrolyte content', says 'customized fluid replacement programs are recommended', and says 'Individual sweat rates can be estimated by measuring body weight before and after exercise.'

Athletes 'should drink a maximum of 80% of the determined sweat loss during a longer period of exercise'. 'activities lasting less than 30-40 minutes, fluid intake is not necessary', and 'endurance sports of up to 60 min can be done without fluid intake'.

### S379
Savoie FA et al., 2015. Effect of hypohydration on muscle endurance, strength, anaerobic power and capacity and vertical jumping ability: a meta-analysis. Sports Med 45(8):1207-27  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-015-0349-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-015-0349-0) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26178327/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26178327&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 28 manuscripts. Muscle endurance -8.3 ± 2.3%, strength -5.5 ± 1.0%, anaerobic power -5.8 ± 2.3%; anaerobic capacity -3.5 ± 2.3% and vertical jump 0.9 ± 0.7% were not significant. No significant correlation with the level of hypohydration. Active dehydration caused an extra 5.4 ± 1.9% loss (P = 0.02). Trained people showed a 3.3 ± 1.7% smaller decrease (P = 0.06). The effect was 'substantially mitigated in trained individuals or when hypohydration is induced passively'.

### S380
Hew-Butler T, Loi V, Pani A, Rosner MH, 2017. Exercise-Associated Hyponatremia: 2017 Update. Front Med (Lausanne) 4:21  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.3389/fmed.2017.00021](https://doi.org/10.3389/fmed.2017.00021) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28316971/>  
Opened: <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5334560/>  
Supports: Diagnostic threshold: any blood [Na+] below 135 mmol/L. 'overdrinking beyond thirst and non-osmotic arginine vasopressin release are the most common etiologic factors'. It quotes the 2015 consensus that 'the primary etiology and mechanism leading to EAH was the overconsumption of hypotonic fluids'. 'Following thirst as a real-time guide appears safe and effective'; 'athletes should not gain weight during the event'; sodium 'cannot prevent EAH in the setting of excessive fluid intake'; 'Sodium-containing sports drinks, which are hypotonic, will not prevent EAH in athletes who overdrink'. The Boston sex difference disappeared after BMI and race-time adjustment.

### S384
He FJ, Li J, MacGregor GA, 2013. Effect of longer term modest salt reduction on blood pressure: Cochrane systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised trials. BMJ 346:f1325

### S385
Huang L et al., 2020. Effect of dose and duration of reduction in dietary sodium on blood pressure levels: systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised trials. BMJ 368:m315  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bmj.m315](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.m315) · Files: nutrition

WHO Regional Office for Europe, 4 Jan 2023. No level of alcohol consumption is safe for our health (news release on WHO statement in Lancet Public Health)

### S393
Kwok A, Dordevic AL, Paton G, Page MJ, Truby H, 2019. Effect of alcohol consumption on food energy intake: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Br J Nutr 121(5):481-495

### S395
Golzarand M, Salari-Moghaddam A, Mirmiran P, 2022. Association between alcohol intake and overweight and obesity: a systematic review and dose-response meta-analysis of 127 observational studies. Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 62(29):8078-8098

Cross-sectional: overweight 1.11, overweight/obesity 1.23, abdominal 1.19, obesity 1.03 (NS). Heavy vs non/light: overweight 1.12, overweight/obesity 1.32, abdominal 1.25. Conclusion: higher odds 'among cross-sectional studies but not cohort studies'.

### S396
Garber CE et al., 2011. ACSM position stand: Quantity and quality of exercise for developing and maintaining cardiorespiratory, musculoskeletal, and neuromotor fitness in apparently healthy adults. Med Sci Sports Exerc  
Type: Position stand · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0b013e318213fefb](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0b013e318213fefb) · Files: cardio-health, strength-hypertrophy  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21694556/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=21694556&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>; full text: <https://acikders.ankara.edu.tr/mod/resource/view.php?id=4289>  
Supports: At least 30 min/day on at least 5 days (at least 150 min/week) moderate or at least 20 min/day on at least 3 days (at least 75 min/week) vigorous; at least 500-1000 MET-min/week; resistance and neuromotor 2-3 days/week; flexibility at least 2 days/week, 60 s per exercise. Full text, Table 5 (p. 1341): very light <30% HRR/VO2R, <57% HRmax, RPE <9; light 30-39, 57-63, RPE 9-11; moderate 40-59, 64-76, RPE 12-13, 3.0-5.9 METs; vigorous 60-89, 77-95, RPE 14-17, 6.0-8.7 METs; near-maximal >=90, >=96, RPE >=18; age-specific moderate METs 4.8-7.1, 4.0-5.9, 3.2-4.7. 'it cannot be assumed that one method of determining exercise intensity is necessarily equivalent to that derived using another method'; HRR or VO2R 'may be preferable'; the advantage 'has not been supported by all studies'.

### S397
Jakicic JM et al., 2019. Association between bout duration of physical activity and health: systematic review. Med Sci Sports Exerc  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000001933](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000001933) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31095078/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=31095078&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 29 articles; activity 'of any bout duration is associated with improved health outcomes, which includes all-cause mortality'; 'Randomized studies only examined bouts of physical activity of at least 10 min.'

### S398
Paluch AE et al., 2022. Daily steps and all-cause mortality: a meta-analysis of 15 international cohorts. Lancet Public Health  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/S2468-2667(21)00302-9](https://doi.org/10.1016/S2468-2667(21)00302-9) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35247352/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=35247352&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=47,471, 3,013 deaths; quartile medians 3,553/5,801/7,842/10,901; HR 0.60 (0.51-0.71), 0.55 (0.49-0.62), 0.47 (0.39-0.57); risk falls until 6,000-8,000 steps/day for age 60 and over and 8,000-10,000 for under 60.

### S399
Ding D et al., 2025. Daily steps and health outcomes in adults: a systematic review and dose-response meta-analysis. Lancet Public Health  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/S2468-2667(25)00164-1](https://doi.org/10.1016/S2468-2667(25)00164-1) · Files: cardio-health

Supports: 57 studies from 35 cohorts (31 meta-analysed); 7,000 vs 2,000 steps: all-cause mortality HR 0.53 (0.46-0.60), CVD incidence 0.75, dementia 0.62 (2 studies), depressive symptoms 0.78, falls 0.72; inflection points around 5,000-7,000; certainty moderate except CVD mortality, cancer incidence and physical function (low) and falls (very low).

### S400
Stens NA et al., 2023. Relationship of daily step counts to all-cause mortality and cardiovascular events. J Am Coll Cardiol 82(15):1483-1494

Supports: 111,309 individuals from 12 studies; significant reduction at 2,517 steps/d (aHR 0.92, 0.84-0.999); optimal 8,763 (aHR 0.40) for mortality and 7,126 (aHR 0.49) for CVD; larger effects with hip-worn accelerometers.

Supports: 17 cohorts, n=226,889 (generally healthy or at CV risk); HR 0.85 (0.81-0.91) per 1,000 steps; 'assesses the effect of up to 20 000 steps/day on outcomes (confirming the more the better)'.

Supports: 8,000 vs 4,000 steps HR 0.49; 12,000 HR 0.35; step intensity not significant after adjusting for steps (HR 0.90, 0.65-1.27).

### S403
Ekelund U et al., 2016. Does physical activity attenuate, or even eliminate, the detrimental association of sitting time with mortality? A harmonised meta-analysis of data from more than 1 million men and women. Lancet

### S406
Shrestha N et al., 2018. Workplace interventions for reducing sitting at work. Cochrane Database Syst Rev

### S409

Vigorous-intensity activity begins at a level of 7 or 8'; 'can talk, but not sing' / 'cannot say more than a few words without pausing for a breath'; moderate 3.0-5.9 METs, vigorous 6.0 or more; relative intensity is a better guide for older adults.

### S524
