Supports: 19 studies, n=765; no significant timing effects on body composition or lipids; morning HbA1c MD -0.11% mainly in T2D; afternoon FBG MD 0.13 mmol/L; certainty moderate to very low

### S566
Iwayama K et al. 2015. Transient energy deficit induced by exercise increases 24-h fat oxidation in young trained men. J Appl Physiol 118(1):80-85  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1152/japplphysiol.00697.2014](https://doi.org/10.1152/japplphysiol.00697.2014) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25554797/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&rettype=abstract&retmode=text&id=25554797>  
Supports: 9 young male endurance athletes, metabolic chamber; 24-h fat oxidation 1,142 (AM) vs 809 (AM/PM) vs 608 (PM) kcal/24 h with similar 24-h EE

### S567
Watson NF et al., 2015, Recommended Amount of Sleep for a Healthy Adult: A Joint Consensus Statement of the AASM and SRS, Sleep  
Type: Position stand · DOI: [10.5665/sleep.4716](https://doi.org/10.5665/sleep.4716) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26039963/>  
Opened: <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4434546/>  
Supports: 'Adults should sleep 7 or more hours per night on a regular basis to promote optimal health'; <7 h associated with weight gain/obesity, diabetes, hypertension, heart disease, stroke, depression, increased risk of death, impaired immune function, accidents; >9 h may be appropriate for young adults, sleep-debt recovery, illness; risk otherwise uncertain.

### S568
Hirshkowitz M et al., 2015, National Sleep Foundation's sleep time duration recommendations: methodology and results summary, Sleep Health  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.1016/j.sleh.2014.12.010](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.sleh.2014.12.010) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29073412/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=29073412&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 18-member panel, RAND/UCLA method: 7-9 h for young adults and adults, 7-8 h for older adults; 'requirements vary across the lifespan and from person to person'.

### S569
Nedeltcheva AV et al., 2010, Insufficient sleep undermines dietary efforts to reduce adiposity, Annals of Internal Medicine  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.7326/0003-4819-153-7-201010050-00006](https://doi.org/10.7326/0003-4819-153-7-201010050-00006) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20921542/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=20921542&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Crossover, n=10 (3 women, 7 men), mean age 41, BMI 27.4; 'decreased the proportion of weight lost as fat by 55% (1.4 vs. 0.6 kg...)' and 'increased the loss of fat-free body mass by 60% (1.5 vs. 2.4 kg)'; increased hunger.

### S570
Wang X et al., 2018, Influence of sleep restriction on weight loss outcomes associated with caloric restriction, Sleep  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1093/sleep/zsy027](https://doi.org/10.1093/sleep/zsy027) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29438540/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=29438540&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=36 (15 CR, 21 CR+SR), 8 weeks; sleep reduction 169 min/week; similar weight, lean and fat loss; proportion lost as fat greater in CR (p=0.016); 'catch-up sleep may not completely reverse it'.

### S571
Al Khatib HK et al., 2017, The effects of partial sleep deprivation on energy balance: a systematic review and meta-analysis, European Journal of Clinical Nutrition  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1038/ejcn.2016.201](https://doi.org/10.1038/ejcn.2016.201) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27804960/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=27804960&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 17 studies (n=496) in the review, 11 (n=172) in the meta-analysis; EI +385 kcal (95% CI 252, 517); no significant change in total EE or RMR; higher fat, lower protein intake.

### S572
Zhu B et al., 2019, Effects of sleep restriction on metabolism-related parameters in healthy adults: a comprehensive review and meta-analysis of RCTs, Sleep Medicine Reviews  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/j.smrv.2019.02.002](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2019.02.002) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30870662/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=30870662&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 41 RCTs; hunger +13.4 mm; +252.8 kcal/d (p=0.011); +0.34 kg; insulin sensitivity SMD -0.70; no strong evidence of an effect on leptin, ghrelin or EE.

### S573
Capers PL et al., 2015, A systematic review and meta-analysis of RCTs of the impact of sleep duration on adiposity and components of energy balance, Obesity Reviews  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1111/obr.12296](https://doi.org/10.1111/obr.12296) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26098388/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26098388&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 18 RCTs; 'sleep restriction increases food intake and total energy expenditure with inconsistent effects on integrated energy balance as operationalized by weight change'; few controlled studies.

### S574
Lin J et al., 2020, Associations of short sleep duration with appetite-regulating hormones and adipokines: a systematic review and meta-analysis, Obesity Reviews  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1111/obr.13051](https://doi.org/10.1111/obr.13051) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32537891/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=32537891&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 21 studies, 2250 participants; ghrelin higher in short sleep (SMD 0.14, 95% CI 0.03-0.25); leptin higher in experimental subgroup (SMD 0.19, 0.03-0.35); sleep deprivation: leptin SMD 0.24, ghrelin SMD 0.18.

### S575
Tasali E et al., 2022, Effect of Sleep Extension on Objectively Assessed Energy Intake Among Adults With Overweight in Real-life Settings: A Randomized Clinical Trial, JAMA Internal Medicine  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1001/jamainternmed.2021.8098](https://doi.org/10.1001/jamainternmed.2021.8098) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35129580/>  
Opened: <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8822469/>  
Supports: n=80, age 21-40, BMI 25-29.9, habitual sleep <6.5 h; 2-week intervention; sleep +1.2 h/night; energy intake -270 kcal/d (95% CI -393 to -147); no TEE effect; weight -0.87 kg (95% CI -1.39 to -0.35) vs control.

### S576
Craven J et al., 2022, Effects of Acute Sleep Loss on Physical Performance: A Systematic and Meta-Analytical Review, Sports Medicine 52(11):2669-2690  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-022-01706-y](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01706-y) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35708888/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=35708888&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 227 outcomes from 69 publications; n=959 (89% male); mean %change -7.56% (95% CI -11.9 to -3.13), I2 98.1%; significant for all categories; consistent effects only with deprivation and late restriction; ~0.4% decrease per hour awake (deprivation and late restriction); PM tasks affected, AM largely unaffected.

### S577
Knowles OE et al., 2018, Inadequate sleep and muscle strength: Implications for resistance training, Journal of Science and Medicine in Sport 21(9):959-968  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1016/j.jsams.2018.01.012](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsams.2018.01.012) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29422383/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=29422383&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 17 studies rated 'moderate' or 'weak'; deprivation had little effect on strength; consecutive nights of restriction could reduce multi-joint but not single-joint force; suggests group training, caffeine, or training before long wakefulness.

### S578
Knowles OE et al., 2022, Sustained Sleep Restriction Reduces Resistance Exercise Quality and Quantity in Females, Medicine & Science in Sports & Exercise 54(12):2167-2177  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000003000](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003000) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36136596/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=36136596&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=10 resistance-trained females, randomised crossover, 9 nights 5 h vs >=7 h TIB: volume load <1% lower; lower-body (not upper-body) compound mean concentric velocity up to 15% slower; squat intraset velocity loss up to 7% greater; cortisol AUC +42%, training distress +84%, sRPE +11%.

### S579
Saner NJ et al., 2020, The effect of sleep restriction, with or without high-intensity interval exercise, on myofibrillar protein synthesis in healthy young men, Journal of Physiology 598(8):1523-1536  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.1113/JP278828](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP278828) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32078168/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=32078168&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: MyoPS 1.24 (SR) vs 1.53 (NS) vs 1.61 (SR+HIIE) %/day, P<0.05; 5 nights 4 h TIB; n=24 young men in matched parallel groups.

### S580
Lamon S et al., 2021, The effect of acute sleep deprivation on skeletal muscle protein synthesis and the hormonal environment, Physiological Reports 9(1):e14660  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.14814/phy2.14660](https://doi.org/10.14814/phy2.14660) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33400856/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=PMC7785053&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Randomised crossover, n=13 (7M/6F); one night total deprivation: postprandial MPS -18% (0.072 vs 0.059 %/h, p=.040), cortisol +21%, testosterone -24%.

### S581
Bartholomew JB et al., 2008, Strength gains after resistance training: the effect of stressful, negative life events, Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research 22(4):1215-21  
Type: Cohort · DOI: [10.1519/JSC.0b013e318173d0bf](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0b013e318173d0bf) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18545186/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=18545186&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=135 undergraduates, 12 weeks, 2x/week; median split; low-stress group had significantly greater bench press and squat 1RM gains; social support unrelated.

### S582
Stults-Kolehmainen MA et al., 2014, Chronic psychological stress impairs recovery of muscular function and somatic sensations over a 96-hour period, Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research 28(7):2007-17  
Type: Cohort · DOI: [10.1519/JSC.0000000000000335](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0000000000000335) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24343323/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=24343323&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=31; life-event stress moderated MIF recovery (linear p=0.027) and energy (p=0.038), fatigue (p=0.040), soreness (p=0.027) over 96 h; perceived stress moderated MIF (p<0.001) and energy (p=0.004) but not fatigue/soreness; adjusted for fitness, workload, training experience.

### S583
Stults-Kolehmainen MA, Bartholomew JB, 2012, Psychological stress impairs short-term muscular recovery from resistance exercise, Medicine & Science in Sports & Exercise 44(11):2220-7  
Type: Cohort · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0b013e31825f67a0](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0b013e31825f67a0) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688829/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=22688829&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=31; life-event stress (P=0.013) and perceived stress (P=0.023) moderated MIF recovery in the first hour; neither moderated energy, fatigue or soreness.

### S584
Edinger JD et al., 2021, Behavioral and psychological treatments for chronic insomnia disorder in adults: an AASM clinical practice guideline, Journal of Clinical Sleep Medicine 17(2):255-262  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.5664/jcsm.8986](https://doi.org/10.5664/jcsm.8986) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33164742/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=33164742&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Multicomponent CBT-I STRONG; stimulus control, sleep restriction and relaxation CONDITIONAL; 'suggest that clinicians not use sleep hygiene as a single-component therapy' (CONDITIONAL).

### S585
Qaseem A et al., 2016, Management of Chronic Insomnia Disorder in Adults: A Clinical Practice Guideline From the American College of Physicians, Annals of Internal Medicine 165(2):125-33  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.7326/M15-2175](https://doi.org/10.7326/M15-2175) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27136449/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=27136449&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: CBT-I as initial treatment for all adults with chronic insomnia (strong recommendation, moderate-quality evidence).

### S586
Irish LA et al., 2015, The role of sleep hygiene in promoting public health: A review of empirical evidence, Sleep Medicine Reviews 22:23-36  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.1016/j.smrv.2014.10.001](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2014.10.001) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25454674/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=25454674&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Notes sleep hygiene is ineffective as a clinical treatment; individual components are associated with sleep, but direct effects in the general population are 'largely untested'.

### S587
Kredlow MA et al., 2015, The effects of physical activity on sleep: a meta-analytic review, Journal of Behavioral Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s10865-015-9617-6](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10865-015-9617-6) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25596964/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=25596964&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 66 studies; regular exercise: small benefits on TST and efficiency, small-to-medium on onset latency, moderate on quality; moderated by sex, age, baseline activity, exercise type, time of day, duration, adherence.

### S588
Kovacevic A et al., 2018, The effect of resistance exercise on sleep: A systematic review of randomized controlled trials, Sleep Medicine Reviews  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1016/j.smrv.2017.07.002](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2017.07.002) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28919335/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=28919335&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 13 studies; chronic resistance exercise improves all aspects of sleep, greatest benefit for quality; benefits attenuated vs aerobic/combined comparators; acute effects poorly studied and inconsistent.

### S589
Wang J et al., 2026, Non-pharmacological interventions for improving sleep quality in adults aged 50 years and older: a systematic review and network meta-analysis, Archives of Public Health  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s13690-026-01892-5](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13690-026-01892-5) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41981687/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=41981687&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 132 RCTs, n=10,872, PSQI>=5; combined aerobic+resistance SMD -1.56 (95% CrI -2.08 to -1.03), the largest effect; only combined training, acupressure and aerobic alone significantly surpassed MCID -0.89; maximal benefit at PSQI ~16 and with daily delivery.

### S590
Stutz J et al., 2019, Effects of Evening Exercise on Sleep in Healthy Participants: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis, Sports Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-018-1015-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-018-1015-0) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30374942/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=30374942&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 23 studies; evening exercise does not negatively affect sleep, 'rather the opposite'; SWS +1.3 pp; SOL, TST and SE might be impaired after vigorous exercise ending <=1 h before bedtime; moderators lost significance after removal of one study.

### S591
Souabni M et al. (last author Saidi O), 2026, Effects of morning versus evening exercise on sleep: a systematic review of comparative studies with a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials, Sleep Medicine Reviews  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/j.smrv.2026.102358](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2026.102358) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42632303/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=42632303&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 16 RCTs meta-analysed; no clear overall morning advantage; evening associated with higher WASO (SMD -0.51 [-0.89, -0.12]); potential small-study effects; differences more apparent at higher intensities; supports flexible timing that prioritises adherence.

### S592
Gardiner C et al., 2023, The effect of caffeine on subsequent sleep: A systematic review and meta-analysis, Sleep Medicine Reviews  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/j.smrv.2023.101764](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2023.101764) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36870101/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=36870101&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 24 studies; TST -45 min, efficiency -7%, onset latency +9 min, WASO +12 min, deep sleep -11.4 min; coffee >=8.8 h and pre-workout (217.5 mg) >=13.2 h before bed.

### S593
Gardiner C et al., 2025, The effect of alcohol on subsequent sleep in healthy adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis, Sleep Medicine Reviews  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/j.smrv.2024.102030](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.smrv.2024.102030) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39631226/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=39631226&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 27 studies; REM disruption from <=0.50 g/kg (~2 drinks), dose-dependent; shorter sleep onset only at >=0.85 g/kg (~5 drinks); TST, efficiency and WASO uncertain.

### S594
Silva AC et al., 2021, Sleep extension in athletes: what we know so far - A systematic review, Sleep Medicine  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1016/j.sleep.2020.11.028](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.sleep.2020.11.028) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33352457/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=33352457&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 2 included studies; extension 26-106 min; 6 of 15 measures large effect, others trivial to medium; risk of bias high for RCT, low for nRCT; evidence quality very low to moderate; conclusions tentative.

### S595
Winwood PW et al., 2026, Tapering and Peaking in the Weight Lifting Sports: A Systematic Review of Athletes' Self-Reported Strategies, Sports Medicine  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-026-02500-w](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-026-02500-w) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42611173/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=42611173&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Taper 5-9 days, volume reduced ~40-45%, cessation 1.5-4 days. Deadlift last done 6-8 days out, squat 5-6.5, bench 3-5.

### S596
Travis SK et al., 2020, Tapering and Peaking Maximal Strength for Powerlifting Performance: A Review, Sports (Basel)  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.3390/sports8090125](https://doi.org/10.3390/sports8090125) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32917000/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=32917000&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Says evidence on peaking for strength athletes is lacking and practitioners must use trial and error.

### S597
Keogh JW, Winwood PW, 2017, The Epidemiology of Injuries Across the Weight-Training Sports, Sports Medicine  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-016-0575-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-016-0575-0) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27328853/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=27328853&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 20 studies, 4 prospective. Bodybuilding 0.24-1/1000 h, strongman 4.5-6.1, Highland Games 7.5. Shoulder, lower back, knee, elbow, wrist/hand most common; strains, tendinitis, sprains most common types. Low rates relative to team sports.

### S598
Tung MJ et al., 2024, Injuries in weightlifting and powerlifting: an updated systematic review, BMJ Open Sport & Exercise Medicine  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1136/bmjsem-2023-001884](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmjsem-2023-001884) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39650568/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=39650568&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Weightlifting 2.4-3.3/1000 h training, powerlifting 1.0-4.4/1000 h; powerlifting point prevalence 70%. Pelvic floor dysfunction 50% in females vs 9.3% in males.

### S599
Serafim TT et al., 2023, Which resistance training is safest to practice? A systematic review, Journal of Orthopaedic Surgery and Research  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1186/s13018-023-03781-x](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13018-023-03781-x) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37046275/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=37046275&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 28 studies (17 CrossFit/HIFT, 3 strength training, 1 strongman). Incidence 0.21-18.9/1000 h. Traditional strength training the safest, strongman the least safe; difficult to generalise.

### S600
Lauersen JB et al., 2014, The effectiveness of exercise interventions to prevent sports injuries: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials, British Journal of Sports Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2013-092538](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2013-092538) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24100287/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=24100287&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 25 trials, 26,610 participants, 3,464 injuries. Stretching RR 0.963 (0.846-1.095). Strength training RR 0.315 (0.207-0.480). Proprioception 0.550, multiple exposures 0.655. Overuse injuries almost halved (RR 0.527).

### S601
Behm DG et al., 2016, Acute effects of muscle stretching on physical performance, range of motion, and injury incidence in healthy active individuals: a systematic review, Applied Physiology, Nutrition, and Metabolism  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1139/apnm-2015-0235](https://doi.org/10.1139/apnm-2015-0235) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26642915/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26642915&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 'SS and PNF stretching had no clear effect on all-cause or overuse injuries; no data are available for DS.' Recommends stretching within a warm-up with post-stretch dynamic activity for reducing muscle injuries.

### S602
Fradkin AJ et al., 2010, Effects of warming-up on physical performance: a systematic review with meta-analysis, Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181c643a0](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181c643a0) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19996770/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=19996770&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 32 studies, all high quality. Warm-up improved performance in 79% of criteria, with little evidence of harm. Few well-conducted RCTs.

### S603
Simic L et al., 2013, Does pre-exercise static stretching inhibit maximal muscular performance? A meta-analytical review, Scandinavian Journal of Medicine & Science in Sports  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1111/j.1600-0838.2012.01444.x](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0838.2012.01444.x) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22316148/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=22316148&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 104 studies. Strength SMD -0.10 (-5.4%), power -0.04 (-1.9%, CI -4.0% to 0.2%), explosive -0.03 (-2.0%). Effects were more pronounced in isometric tests, smallest at 45 s or less of stretch, and unrelated to age, sex or fitness. Advises against static stretching as the sole warm-up activity.

### S604
Alizadeh S et al., 2023, Resistance Training Induces Improvements in Range of Motion: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis, Sports Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-022-01804-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01804-x) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36622555/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=36622555&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 55 studies. ES 0.73 for resistance training on ROM. RT vs stretch ES 0.08 (p=0.79). RT+stretch vs stretch ES -0.001. Untrained 1.042 vs trained 0.43. Bodyweight-only not significant. No effect of age, duration or frequency.

### S605
Afonso J et al., 2021, Strength Training versus Stretching for Improving Range of Motion: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis, Healthcare (Basel)  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.3390/healthcare9040427](https://doi.org/10.3390/healthcare9040427) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33917036/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=33917036&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 11 RCTs, n=452. ES -0.22 (95% CI -0.55 to 0.12), p=0.206, no difference between ST and stretching. Highly heterogeneous studies.

### S606
Arntz F et al., 2024, Chronic Effects of Static Stretching Exercises on Skeletal Muscle Hypertrophy in Healthy Individuals: A Systematic Review and Multilevel Meta-Analysis, Sports Medicine - Open  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s40798-024-00772-y](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40798-024-00772-y) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39340744/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=39340744&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: SMD 0.118 (95% PI -0.233 to 0.469), described as an unclear effect. Concludes static stretching 'may not be highly effective... unless a higher duration of training is utilized'.

### S607
Warneke K et al., 2024, Effects of Chronic Static Stretching on Maximal Strength and Muscle Hypertrophy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis with Meta-Regression, Sports Medicine - Open  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s40798-024-00706-8](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40798-024-00706-8) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38637473/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=38637473&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 42 studies, 1,318 participants. Strength d=0.30, hypertrophy d=0.20. Lower dosage not significant (p=0.13-0.39). 'Of minor effectiveness'.

### S608
van der Vlist AC et al., 2021, Which treatment is most effective for patients with Achilles tendinopathy? A living systematic review with network meta-analysis of 29 randomised controlled trials, British Journal of Sports Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2019-101872](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2019-101872) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32522732/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=32522732&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 29 RCTs, 76% at high risk of bias. Any treatment class seemed superior to wait-and-see at 3 months (very low to low certainty). No clinically relevant differences between active treatments. 'Clinicians could consider starting treatment with a calf-muscle exercise programme.'

### S609
Wilson F et al., 2018, Exercise, orthoses and splinting for treating Achilles tendinopathy: a systematic review with meta-analysis, British Journal of Sports Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2017-098913](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2017-098913) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30170996/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=30170996&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 22 studies, 1,137 participants. Eccentric better than control (moderate evidence) and than concentric for pain. No difference between eccentric and HSR. Conditional recommendation for exercise, with no one programme favoured.

### S610
Liu Y et al., 2026, Comparative effectiveness of exercise interventions for patellar tendinopathy: a systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials, BMC Sports Science, Medicine and Rehabilitation  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s13102-026-01743-4](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13102-026-01743-4) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42192475/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=42192475&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 17 RCTs, primary network of 10 studies with n=313. No intervention statistically superior to HSR. Flywheel, eccentric step and concentric training estimated worse than HSR. Most trials had some concerns; none at overall high risk. HSR remains a reasonable reference treatment.

### S611
Smith BE et al., 2017, Should exercises be painful in the management of chronic musculoskeletal pain? A systematic review and meta-analysis, British Journal of Sports Medicine  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2016-097383](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2016-097383) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28596288/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=28596288&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 7 trials, n=385. Short-term ES -0.27 (-0.54 to -0.05) favouring painful exercise. No difference medium/long term. 'Pain... need not be a barrier.'

### S612
Silbernagel KG et al., 2007, Continued sports activity, using a pain-monitoring model, during rehabilitation in patients with Achilles tendinopathy: a randomized controlled study, American Journal of Sports Medicine  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1177/0363546506298279](https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546506298279) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17307888/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=17307888&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: n=38. No difference between continued pain-monitored activity and active rest. VISA-A 57 to 85 vs 57 to 91. 'No negative effects could be demonstrated.'

### S613
World Health Organization, 2023, WHO guideline for non-surgical management of chronic primary low back pain in adults in primary and community care settings (summary brochure)  
Type: Guideline · DOI: none · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789240081789>  
Opened: <https://cdn.who.int/media/docs/default-source/mca-documents/ageing/lbp/summary_who_guideline_lower-back-pain_brochure_v7.pdf>  
Supports: Table: structured exercise therapies or programmes may be offered (b = low certainty). Traction, lumbar braces/belts/supports should not be used routinely (c = very low). Opioids not routine (a = moderate). Chronic primary LBP is over 90% of presentations.

### S614
Hayden JA et al., 2021, Exercise therapy for chronic low back pain, Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1002/14651858.CD009790.pub2](https://doi.org/10.1002/14651858.CD009790.pub2) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34580864/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=34580864&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 249 trials. Pain MD -15.2 (-18.3 to -12.2), moderate certainty. Function MD -6.8, not clinically important. No difference vs manual therapy. Mostly minor harms.

### S615
Foster NE et al., 2018, Prevention and treatment of low back pain: evidence, challenges, and promising directions, The Lancet  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.1016/S0140-6736(18)30489-6](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(18)30489-6) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29573872/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=29573872&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Guidelines recommend education, self-management, resuming normal activities and exercise. Notes inappropriately high use of imaging, rest, opioids, injections and surgery.

### S616
Saraceni N et al., 2020, To Flex or Not to Flex? Is There a Relationship Between Lumbar Spine Flexion During Lifting and Low Back Pain? A Systematic Review With Meta-analysis, Journal of Orthopaedic & Sports Physical Therapy  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.2519/jospt.2020.9218](https://doi.org/10.2519/jospt.2020.9218) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31775556/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=31775556&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: No difference in peak lumbar flexion (1.5°, -0.7 to 3.7). People with LBP showed 6.0° less flexion (thoracopelvic). 9/11 studies showed no difference. Low-quality evidence that flexion is not a risk factor.

### S617
Michie S et al., 2009, Effective techniques in healthy eating and physical activity interventions: a meta-regression, Health Psychology 28(6):690-701  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1037/a0016136](https://doi.org/10.1037/a0016136) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19916637/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=19916637&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 122 evaluations, N=44,747, pooled ES 0.31 (0.26-0.36), I2 69%; self-monitoring explained the greatest heterogeneity (13%); self-monitoring + at least one other control-theory technique 0.42 vs 0.26

### S618
Samdal GB et al., 2017, Effective behaviour change techniques for physical activity and healthy eating in overweight and obese adults; systematic review and meta-regression analyses, Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act 14:42  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s12966-017-0494-y](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12966-017-0494-y) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28351367/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=28351367&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 48 RCTs, mean age >=40, BMI >=30; long-term ES 0.24 (I2 59.4%), short-term ES 0.37 (I2 71.3%); goal setting and self-monitoring of behaviour predicted effect at short and long term

### S619
French DP et al., 2014, Which behaviour change techniques are most effective at increasing older adults' self-efficacy and physical activity behaviour? A systematic review, Ann Behav Med 48(2):225-34  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1007/s12160-014-9593-z](https://doi.org/10.1007/s12160-014-9593-z) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24648017/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=24648017&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 24 studies in adults 60+; goal setting, prompting self-monitoring, relapse planning, feedback associated with lower self-efficacy and PA

### S620
McEwan D et al., 2016, The effectiveness of multi-component goal setting interventions for changing physical activity behaviour: a systematic review and meta-analysis, Health Psychology Review 10(1):67-88

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26445201&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 45 articles, 52 interventions, n=5912; Cohen's d=0.552, 95% CI 0.43-0.67; effective across diverse populations and settings

### S621
Ferguson T et al., 2022, Effectiveness of wearable activity trackers to increase physical activity and improve health: a systematic review of systematic reviews and meta-analyses, Lancet Digital Health

Supports: 39 reviews, 163,992 participants; PA SMD 0.3-0.6, ~1800 steps/day, ~40 min/day walking, ~1 kg weight; benefit sustained

### S622
Feil K, Fritsch J, Rhodes RE, 2023, The intention-behaviour gap in physical activity: a systematic review and meta-analysis of the action control framework, British Journal of Sports Medicine 57(19):1265-1271  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2022-106640](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2022-106640) · Files: recovery-habits

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=37460164&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 25 samples, N=29,600; unsuccessful intenders 33.0%, successful 38.7%; gap 47.6%; 'nearly at chance'

### S623
Rhodes RE, de Bruijn GJ, 2013, How big is the physical activity intention-behaviour gap? A meta-analysis using the action control framework, British Journal of Health Psychology 18(2):296-309  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1111/bjhp.12032](https://doi.org/10.1111/bjhp.12032) · Files: recovery-habits

Supports: 10 studies, N=3,899; unsuccessful intenders 36%, successful 42%; overall gap 46% (48% in contribution statement)

### S624
Bélanger-Gravel A, Godin G, Amireault S, 2013 (online 2011), A meta-analytic review of the effect of implementation intentions on physical activity, Health Psychology Review 7(1):23-54

Peng S et al., 2022, The Effectiveness of Planning Interventions for Improving Physical Activity in the General Population: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials, Int J Environ Res Public Health 19(12):7337

Supports: 41 trials in SR, 35 in MA; SMD 0.35 (0.25-0.44), I2 61.4%; more effective among patients, males, action planning, face-to-face, reinforcement during follow-up

### S626
Silva MAV et al., 2018, Impact of implementation intentions on physical activity practice in adults: systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized clinical trials, PLoS One 13(11):e0206294

Supports: 13 RCTs, ages 18-76; reinforced-implementation-intention subgroup 0.25 (95% CI 0.05-0.45)

### S627
Singh B, Murphy A, Maher C, Smith AE, 2024, Time to Form a Habit: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Health Behaviour Habit Formation and Its Determinants, Healthcare (Basel) 12(23):2488

Supports: 20 studies, 2601 participants; 11 high risk of bias; four studies reported times: 59-66 days median, 106-154 days mean, range 4-335 days; SMD 0.69 (0.49-0.88); 'Emerging evidence'. Full text (PMC11641623): Lally 2010 median 66 days (range 18-254) to 95% automaticity.

### S628
Lally P, van Jaarsveld CHM, Potts HWW, Wardle J, 2010 (online 2009), How are habits formed: Modelling habit formation in the real world, European Journal of Social Psychology 40(6):998-1009

Opened: <https://api.crossref.org/works/10.1002/ejsp.674>  
Supports: 96 volunteers, 82 analysed, model fitted for 62 (39 good fit); time to 95% of asymptote 18-254 days; missing one opportunity did not materially affect habit formation

### S629
Kaushal N, Rhodes RE, 2015, Exercise habit formation in new gym members: a longitudinal study, J Behav Med 38(4):652-63  
Type: Cohort · DOI: [10.1007/s10865-015-9640-7](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10865-015-9640-7) · Files: recovery-habits

### S630
Ntoumanis N et al., 2021, A meta-analysis of self-determination theory-informed intervention studies in the health domain: effects on motivation, health behavior, physical, and psychological health, Health Psychology Review 15(2):214-244

Supports: 73 studies; small-to-medium changes in SDT constructs and health behaviours at end and follow-up; small changes in physical/psychological health; need support and autonomous (not controlled) motivation associated with behaviour change; effects modest, heterogeneous

### S631
Teixeira PJ et al., 2012, Exercise, physical activity, and self-determination theory: a systematic review, Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act 9:78

### S632
Rhodes RE, Kates A, 2015, Can the Affective Response to Exercise Predict Future Motives and Physical Activity Behavior? A Systematic Review of Published Evidence, Ann Behav Med 49(5):715-31

Collado-Mateo D et al., 2021, Key Factors Associated with Adherence to Physical Exercise in Patients with Chronic Diseases and Older Adults: An Umbrella Review, Int J Environ Res Public Health 18(4):2023

### S634
Sperandei S, Vieira MC, Reis AC, 2016, Adherence to physical activity in an unsupervised setting: Explanatory variables for high attrition rates among fitness center members, J Sci Med Sport 19(11):916-920

### S635
Vancampfort D et al., 2021, Dropout from exercise randomized controlled trials among people with anxiety and stress-related disorders: A meta-analysis and meta-regression, J Affect Disord 282:996-1004

URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33601745/>

Supports: 14 RCTs, n=369; dropout 22.4% (15.0-32.0%); controlled motivation strategies -> higher dropout; supervision of all sessions by exercise expert and autonomous motivation strategies -> lower dropout; similar to control (OR 0.84)

### S636
Madigan CD et al., 2015, Is self-weighing an effective tool for weight loss: a systematic literature review and meta-analysis, Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s12966-015-0267-4](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12966-015-0267-4) · Files: recovery-habits

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=26293454&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Self-weighing alone -0.5 kg (-1.3 to 0.3); added to programme -1.7 kg (-2.6 to -0.8); no daily vs weekly difference; accountability p=0.03

### S637
Varkevisser RDM et al., 2019, Determinants of weight loss maintenance: a systematic review, Obesity Reviews

Supports: 67 articles (prospective studies, 2006-2016); demographics consistently not predictive; behavioural/cognitive determinants reducing intake, increasing expenditure and monitoring this balance are predictive

### S638
Paixão C et al., 2020, Successful weight loss maintenance: A systematic review of weight control registries, Obesity Reviews

Supports: 52 articles, 5 registries (US, Portugal, Germany, Finland, Greece); increased physical activity the most consistent positive correlate of maintenance

### S639
Teixeira PJ et al., 2015, Successful behavior change in obesity interventions in adults: a systematic review of self-regulation mediators, BMC Medicine  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1186/s12916-015-0323-6](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12916-015-0323-6) · Files: recovery-habits

### S640

Supports: 29 studies; at 5 years average maintained >3 kg and >3% reduced weight; HBD 2.0 kg (1.5-2.5), 2.1% reduced weight vs VLED 7.1 kg; six studies: more exercise -> greater maintenance

Warburton D, Jamnik V, Bredin SSD, Shephard RJ, Gledhill N. The 2023 Physical Activity Readiness Questionnaire for Everyone (PAR-Q+) and electronic Physical Activity Readiness Medical Examination (ePARmed-X+). Health & Fitness Journal of Canada 2023;16(1):46-49

Landing page: 'supersedes all previous editions'.

Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.1097/AOG.0000000000003772](https://doi.org/10.1097/AOG.0000000000003772) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32217980/>  
Opened: <https://www.acog.org/womens-health/faqs/exercise-during-pregnancy>  
Supports: FAQ 'What are warning signs that I should stop exercising?': bleeding from the vagina, feeling dizzy or faint, shortness of breath before starting exercise, chest pain, headache, muscle weakness, calf pain or swelling, regular painful contractions of the uterus, fluid gushing or leaking from the vagina; 'If you have any of them, stop and call your ob-gyn.' CO 804 PubMed abstract also opened.

### S708
Evenson KR, Barakat R, Brown WJ et al., 2014. Guidelines for Physical Activity during Pregnancy: Comparisons From Around the World. Am J Lifestyle Med 8(2):102-121  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.1177/1559827613498204](https://doi.org/10.1177/1559827613498204) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4206837/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=4206837&rettype=xml>  
Supports: '11 guidelines were identified from nine countries'; 'Six guidelines included indications for stopping exercise... All six guidelines indicated ceasing exercise with dizziness or presyncope, dyspnea before exertion, or vaginal bleeding. Five of the six ... abdominal or back pain, amniotic fluid leakage, or excessive shortness of breath.' In Table 5, decreased fetal movement is marked in 4 guidelines; the table also lists calf pain/swelling, chest pain and headache.

### S709
Prevett C, Gingerich J, Sivak A, Davenport MH, 2025. Resistance training in pregnancy: systematic review and meta-analysis of pregnancy, delivery, fetal and pelvic floor outcomes and call to action. Br J Sports Med 59(16):1173-1182  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2024-109123](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2024-109123) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40610191/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=40610191&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 50 studies (47,619 participants), 45 RCTs; gestational hypertension OR 0.42 (0.27-0.66), GDM OR 0.62 (0.48-0.79), perinatal mood disorders OR 0.48 (0.32-0.73), macrosomia OR 0.67 (0.50-0.88); 'Ninety per cent of studies were multicomponent programmes, and the dosage of RT interventions was generally low-to-moderate'.

### S710
Prevett C, Kimber ML, Forner L, de Vivo M, Davenport MH, 2023. Impact of heavy resistance training on pregnancy and postpartum health outcomes. Int Urogynecol J 34(2):405-411  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.1007/s00192-022-05393-1](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00192-022-05393-1) · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36331580/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=36331580&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Online survey, n=679 lifting >=80% 1RM; CrossFit 61% (410/675); 66% (388/589) no complications; 57% (241/426) UI after pregnancy; maintainers fewer reproductive complications (p=0.006); Olympic lifting 72%, supine 71%, Valsalva 34%; outcomes 'not altered whether they engaged in, or avoided Olympic lifting, Valsalva or supine weightlifting'.

### S711
Prevett C, Davenport MH, 2026. High-Load Resistance Training in the First Trimester, a Retrospective Survey: Implications for Exercise Professionals and Obstetrical Providers. Med Sci Sports Exerc 58(6):1236-1245  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.1249/MSS.0000000000003956](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003956) · Files: recovery-habits

Supports: n=359; frequency 4.8 to 4.0 d/wk; Olympic weightlifting continued n=127/159 (80%); load increased or maintained n=126/203 (62%); fatigue/nausea 77%; 10 (3%) miscarriage during enrolment; 41 logs >75% e1RM; 'well tolerated'.

### S712
Moolyk AN, Wilson MK, Matenchuk BA, Bains G, Gervais MJ, Wowdzia JB, Davenport MH, 2025. Maternal and fetal responses to acute high-intensity resistance exercise during pregnancy. Br J Sports Med 59(3):159-166

URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39694629/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=39694629&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 10 pregnant (26.4±3.2 wk) vs 10 non-pregnant; squat/bench/deadlift at 70-90% 10RM plus 90% with Valsalva; maternal HR peaked with Valsalva (squat 137.3±8.4 bpm); 'Fetal bradycardia was not observed'; FHR unchanged pre to post; umbilical RI 'remained within normal ranges'; 'well tolerated ... including while using the Valsalva manoeuvre'.

### S713
Meah VL, Strynadka MC, Steinback CD, Davenport MH, 2021. Cardiac Responses to Prenatal Resistance Exercise with and without the Valsalva Maneuver. Med Sci Sports Exerc 53(6):1260-1269

Supports: 15 pregnant (22.9±5.9 wk) vs 15 non-pregnant; semi-reclined leg press 20/40/60% 10RM and 40% with VM; pregnant had greater ejection fraction during free-breathing RE, otherwise haemodynamics not different; 'No differences were observed between groups during 40% 10RM RE with and without VM'; 'proportionate cardiac responses'.

### S714
Mottola MF, Nagpal TS, Bgeginski R et al., 2019. Is supine exercise associated with adverse maternal and fetal outcomes? A systematic review. Br J Sports Med 53(2):82-89

Supports: Seven studies (n=1759); very low to low quality; no association with low birth weight; abnormal FHR in 20 of 65 (31%) during acute supine exercise; UBF decreased 13%; 'insufficient evidence to ascertain whether maternal exercise in the supine position is safe or should be avoided'.

### S715
Worska A et al., 2024. New Public Health and Sport Medicine Institutions Guidelines of Physical Activity Intensity for Pregnancy - A Scoping Review. J Clin Med 13(6):1738

Supports: Scoping review of 22 guidelines. 'The most frequently recommended methods ... are the rating of perceived exertion, the Talk Test, and heart rate measurements'; 'For moderate PA, ratings should be 13 to 14 (somewhat hard)'; 'because blunted and normal HR responses to PA have been reported ... the RPE scale may be a more effective means'; 140 bpm cap 'removed in the next update from ACOG in 1994' but still used.

Canadian Society for Exercise Physiology. Get Active Questionnaire for Pregnancy (GAQ-P), 2021

Supports: Moderate: 'You can talk, but could not sing'; vigorous: 'cannot say more than a few words without pausing to breathe'; consult a health care provider before vigorous activity or activity above 2500 m.

### S717
Beetham KS et al., 2019. The effects of vigorous intensity exercise in the third trimester of pregnancy: a systematic review and meta-analysis. BMC Pregnancy Childbirth 19(1):281  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1186/s12884-019-2441-1](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12884-019-2441-1) · Files: recovery-habits

Supports: 10 cohorts (n=32,080) + 5 RCTs (n=623); birthweight MD 8.06 g (not significant); no increased SGA or LBW risk; gestational age +0.21 wk; reduced prematurity; 'appears to be safe for most healthy pregnancies'; further research needed on intensity exceeding 90% HRmax.

### S718
Salvesen KA, Hem E, Sundgot-Borgen J, 2012. Fetal wellbeing may be compromised during strenuous exercise among pregnant elite athletes. Br J Sports Med 46(4):279-283

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=21393257&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 6 Olympic-level athletes at 23-29 wk; FHR normal below 90% maternal HRmax; fetal bradycardia and high umbilical artery PI above 90% HRmax with uterine flow below 50% of baseline; both normalised quickly after stopping.

### S719
Ravanelli N, Casasola W, English T, Edwards KM, Jay O, 2019. Heat stress and fetal risk. Environmental limits for exercise and passive heat stress during pregnancy: a systematic review with best evidence synthesis. Br J Sports Med 53(13):799-805

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=29496695&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 12 studies (n=347); no woman exceeded 39.0°C; highest 38.9°C; highest land-based mean 38.3°C; safe: 35 min at 80-90% HRmax at 25°C/45% RH, water immersion ≤33.4°C for 45 min, baths 40°C or saunas 70°C/15% RH for up to 20 min.

### S720
Smallcombe JW et al., 2021. Thermoregulation During Pregnancy: a Controlled Trial Investigating the Risk of Maternal Hyperthermia During Exercise in the Heat. Sports Med 51(12):2655-2664

Supports: 15 pregnant vs 15 non-pregnant; 45 min moderate exercise at 32°C/45% RH; highest rectal temperature 37.93°C; 'No thermoregulatory impairments with pregnancy were observed'; pregnant women felt hotter in the WB trial; cites epidemiological evidence linking heat exposure to negative birth outcomes.

### S721
Canadian Society for Exercise Physiology, 2025. 2025 Canadian Guideline for Physical Activity, Sedentary Behaviour & Sleep Throughout the First Year Postpartum  
Type: Guideline · DOI: none · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://csepguidelines.ca/wp-content/uploads/2025/03/2025-Canadian-Guideline-for-Physical-Activity-Sedentary-Behaviour-and-Sleep-throughout-the-First-Year-Postpartum_EN.pdf>  
Supports: Rec 3: at least 120 min MVPA over 4+ days incl. aerobic and resistance; Rec 5: begin or return to MVPA in the first 12 weeks; Rec 6: early light activity, MVPA once incisions/tears healed and lochia does not increase; Rec 7: individualised, gradual, symptom-based; odds reductions 45%/37%/28%; no increased harm incl. breast milk.

### S722
Davenport MH et al., 2025. International Delphi study of clinical and exercise professionals' opinion of physical activity prescreening and contraindications for participating in postpartum physical activity. Br J Sports Med 59(8):527-538

URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40054885/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=40054885&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Rounds of 120/105/95 participants; consensus (≥75%) on 46/49 statements; 24 relative contraindications incl. loss of consciousness, neurological symptoms, kidney disease, calf pain/swelling suggestive of DVT, severe abdominal pain, vaginal bleeding, cardiomyopathy, caesarean symptoms worsening with MVPA, unstable hypertension, chest pain, dizziness; basis of the Get Active Questionnaire for Postpartum.

### S723
Goom T, Donnelly G, Brockwell E, 2019. Returning to running postnatal - guidelines for medical, health and fitness professionals managing this population  
Type: Guideline · DOI: none · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://athleticsni.org/download/files/Returning_to_running_postnatal_guideline_for_medical_health_and_fitness_professionals_managing_this_population.05.pdf>  
Supports: Evidence-Based Recommendation 2 (Level 4): 'Return to running is not advisable prior to 3 months postnatal or beyond this if any symptoms of pelvic floor dysfunction'. 'Low impact exercise timeline ... within the first 3 months ... followed by a return to running between 3-6 months postnatal, at the earliest'. Load-and-impact tests: walk 30 min, SL balance 10 s, SL squat 10 each side, jog 1 min, 10 bounds, 10 hops each leg, running man 10 each side, all without pain, heaviness, dragging or incontinence. Strength tests: 20 reps each; 'Weakness ... should not be considered a barrier'. Table 2 risk factors.

Kimber ML et al., 2021. Health Outcomes after Pregnancy in Elite Athletes: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis. Med Sci Sports Exerc 53(8):1739-1747

### S726
Woodley SJ et al., 2020. Pelvic floor muscle training for preventing and treating urinary and faecal incontinence in antenatal and postnatal women. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 5:CD007471

Moser O et al. Glucose management for exercise using continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) and intermittently scanned CGM (isCGM) systems in type 1 diabetes: position statement of the EASD and ISPAD. Pediatr Diabetes 2020;21(8):1375-1393

### S744
Baffour-Awuah B et al. An evidence-based guide to the efficacy and safety of isometric resistance training in hypertension and clinical implications. Clin Hypertens 2023;29(1):9  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.1186/s40885-022-00232-3](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40885-022-00232-3) · Files: recovery-habits

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=10015931&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Table: IRT 30% MVC BP rises 20-30 (normotensive) / 40-50 (hypertensive). 'Contractions 50% MVC, or above, are likely to generate a notable pressor (hypertensive) response >200/100 mmHg'. Handgrip 30% MVC 4x2 min, 3-min rest, 'Do not exceed 30% MVC'. Wall squat 15-25% MVC 4x2 min, 2-min rest. 'Breathe normally (to avoid Valsalva pressor response)'. Red flags: unstable angina, uncontrolled grade II+ HTN, arrhythmia. No IRT-related adverse events reported.

Lilja M et al., 2018, High doses of anti-inflammatory drugs compromise muscle strength and hypertrophic adaptations to resistance training in young adults, Acta Physiol (Oxf) 222(2):e12948  
Type: RCT · DOI: [10.1111/apha.12948](https://doi.org/10.1111/apha.12948) · Files: recovery-habits

Supports: Healthy men and women aged 18-35; ibuprofen 1200 mg/day (n=15) vs ASA 75 mg (n=16) for 8 wk; quadriceps volume +3.7% vs +7.5% (P=0.029).

### S773
Trappe TA et al., 2011, Influence of acetaminophen and ibuprofen on skeletal muscle adaptations to resistance exercise in older adults, Am J Physiol Regul Integr Comp Physiol 300(3):R655-662

Masuda Y et al., 2023, Clinical characteristics and outcomes of exertional rhabdomyolysis after indoor spinning: a systematic review, Phys Sportsmed 51(4):294-305

Fahey M et al., 2022. The Value of High Intensity Locomotor Training Applied to Patients With Acute-Onset Neurologic Injury. Arch Phys Med Rehabil 103(7S):S178-S188

Supports: Table 1 reproduces the ACSM GETP 10th edition (2018) bands: very light <30% HRR, <57% HRmax, RPE <9; light 30-39, 57-63, 9-11; moderate 40-59, 64-76, 12-13; hard/vigorous 60-84, 77-95, 14-17; very hard to maximal 85-100, 96-100, 18-20. A secondary transcription in a neurorehabilitation paper, not the GETP book.

### S777
Swain DP, Leutholtz BC, 1997. Heart rate reserve is equivalent to %VO2 reserve, not to %VO2max. Med Sci Sports Exerc 29(3):410-414  
Type: Other · DOI: [10.1097/00005768-199703000-00018](https://doi.org/10.1097/00005768-199703000-00018) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9139182/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=9139182&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 63 adults, cycle ergometry: %HRR vs %VO2max intercept -11.6, slope 1.12 (differed from identity); %HRR vs %VO2R intercept -0.1, slope 1.00 (not different from identity); the %HRR-%VO2max gap was larger in less fit people.

### S778
Swain DP, Leutholtz BC, King ME, Haas LA, Branch JD, 1998. Relationship between % heart rate reserve and % VO2 reserve in treadmill exercise. Med Sci Sports Exerc 30(2):318-321

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=9502363&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 50 adults, treadmill: %HRR vs %VO2max intercept -6.1, slope 1.10; %HRR vs %VO2R intercept 1.5, slope 1.03, significantly closer to identity (P<0.001).

### S779
Swain DP, Abernathy KS, Smith CS, Lee SJ, Bunn SA, 1994. Target heart rates for the development of cardiorespiratory fitness. Med Sci Sports Exerc 26(1):112-116

Supports: 81 men and 81 women aged 18-34: 40, 60, 80 and 85% VO2max corresponded to 63, 76, 89 and 92% HRmax, all significantly greater (P<0.001) than the ACSM values of 55, 70, 85 and 90% HRmax.

### S780
Iannetta D et al., 2020. A Critical Evaluation of Current Methods for Exercise Prescription in Women and Men. Med Sci Sports Exerc 52(2):466-473

Supports: 100 adults (46 women): lactate threshold at 45-74% VO2max and 60-90% HRmax; MLSS at 69-96% VO2max and 75-97% HRmax; fixed percentages 'conform poorly to exercise intensity domains'.

### S781
Milani JGPO et al., 2026. Cardiorespiratory Exercise Intensity Prescription in Cardiovascular Rehabilitation: Do Updated Guideline Recommendations Reflect Real Individual Effort Responses? Eur J Prev Cardiol (online ahead of print)

Supports: 2,554 patients with cardiometabolic disease, 12 centres: HR at VT1 close to the 40% HRR lower limit (mean absolute error 6.4 bpm); HR at VT2 substantially above the 59% HRR upper limit (bias -11.4 bpm, mean absolute error 12.0 bpm).

### S782
Mann T, Lamberts RP, Lambert MI, 2013. Methods of prescribing relative exercise intensity: physiological and practical considerations. Sports Med 43(7):613-625  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: [10.1007/s40279-013-0045-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-013-0045-x) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23620244/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=23620244&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: At the same %VO2max or %HRmax some individuals are above and others below metabolic thresholds; similar inter-individual variation in blood lactate at the same %VO2R or %HRR; thresholds need verification trials and vary more day to day; many theoretical advantages of threshold-based prescription 'have yet to be directly demonstrated'.

### S783
Ghadieh AS, Saab B, 2015. Evidence for exercise training in the management of hypertension in adults. Can Fam Physician 61(3):233-239  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: none · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25927108/>  
Opened: <https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4369613/>  
Supports: Table of contraindications (attributed to ACSM, AHA and ACC): 'uncontrolled severe hypertension (BP >=180/110 mm Hg)' is an absolute contraindication to aerobic and resistance training.

### S784
Brooke-Wavell K et al., 2022. Strong, steady and straight: UK consensus statement on physical activity and exercise for osteoporosis. Br J Sports Med 56(15):837-846  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.1136/bjsports-2021-104634](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2021-104634) · Files: strength-hypertrophy, cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35577538/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=9304091&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Resistance and impact exercise to maximise bone strength; muscle strengthening 2 or 3 days a week, progressive resistance building up to 3 sets of the maximum load that can be lifted 8-12 times [E]; moderate impact (stamping, jogging, low-level jumping, hopping) on most days, at least 50 impacts per session, e.g. 5 sets of 10 [C]; combined impact and progressive resistance best; Australian guidance gives 50 jumps in 3-5 sets on 4-7 days at more than 4 times body weight for those without osteoporosis; little evidence of significant harm, benefits generally outweigh risks; with vertebral fracture or multiple low-trauma fractures, impact usually only up to brisk walking.

### S785
Beck BR, Daly RM, Singh MA, Taaffe DR, 2017. Exercise and Sports Science Australia (ESSA) position statement on exercise prescription for the prevention and management of osteoporosis. J Sci Med Sport 20(5):438-445  
Type: Position stand · DOI: [10.1016/j.jsams.2016.10.001](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsams.2016.10.001) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27840033/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=27840033&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Bone responds positively to impact activities and high intensity progressive resistance training; loaded spine flexion is not recommended; covers prevention and management of osteoporosis.

### S786
Howe TE et al., 2011. Exercise for preventing and treating osteoporosis in postmenopausal women. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2011(7):CD000333  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.1002/14651858.CD000333.pub2](https://doi.org/10.1002/14651858.CD000333.pub2) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21735380/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=21735380&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 43 RCTs, 4320 participants: progressive resistance training for the lower limbs best for femoral neck BMD (MD 1.03, 95% CI 0.24 to 1.82); combination programmes best for spine (MD 3.22, 95% CI 1.80 to 4.64); fractures OR 0.61 (95% CI 0.23 to 1.64), not significant.

### S787
Zhao R, Zhao M, Xu Z, 2015. The effects of differing resistance training modes on the preservation of bone mineral density in postmenopausal women: a meta-analysis. Osteoporos Int 26(5):1605-1618  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1007/s00198-015-3034-0](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00198-015-3034-0) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25603795/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=25603795&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 24 studies: resistance training raised femoral neck BMD (SMD 0.303) and lumbar spine BMD (SMD 0.311); combined resistance with high-impact or weight-bearing programmes did better (hip SMD 0.411, spine SMD 0.431); resistance alone was not significant.

### S788
Zhou Z et al., 2026. Effects of different types of exercise over 24 weeks on bone mineral density in postmenopausal women: a systematic review with pairwise and network meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. J Sport Health Sci 15:101127  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1016/j.jshs.2026.101127](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jshs.2026.101127) · Files: cardio-health  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41617082/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=41617082&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: 74 RCTs, 5331 participants, interventions of at least 24 weeks: site- and modality-specific effects; more consistent gains with interventions of 52 weeks or more; similar gains with normal and low BMD; mind-body exercise plus resistance training ranked highly at several sites.

### S789
Ma D, Wu L, He Z, 2013. Effects of walking on the preservation of bone mineral density in perimenopausal and postmenopausal women: a systematic review and meta-analysis. Menopause 20(11):1216-1226

Supports: 10 trials: walking alone had no significant effect on lumbar spine BMD (WMD 0.01 g/cm2, 95% CI -0.00 to 0.02), radius or whole body; no femoral neck effect overall (WMD 0.01, 95% CI -0.00 to 0.01), with a positive effect only after interventions longer than 6 months.

### S790
Watson SL et al., 2018. High-Intensity Resistance and Impact Training Improves Bone Mineral Density and Physical Function in Postmenopausal Women With Osteopenia and Osteoporosis: The LIFTMOR Randomized Controlled Trial. J Bone Miner Res 33(2):211-220

Supports: 101 women aged 65 +/- 5, T-score < -1.0, screened: 8 months of twice-weekly 30-minute supervised training (5 sets of 5 at >85% 1RM, plus impact): lumbar spine BMD 2.9 vs -1.2% and femoral neck 0.3 vs -1.9% versus a home programme; one minor adverse event (lower back spasm).

### S791
Watson SL et al., 2019. High-intensity exercise did not cause vertebral fractures and improves thoracic kyphosis in postmenopausal women with low to very low bone mass: the LIFTMOR trial. Osteoporos Int 30(5):957-964

### S792
O'Bryan SJ et al., 2022. Progressive Resistance Training for Concomitant Increases in Muscle Strength and Bone Mineral Density in Older Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis. Sports Med 52(8):1939-1960

### S793
Hamilton BR et al., 2022. The Effects of Exercise on Bone Mineral Density in Men: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomised Controlled Trials. Calcif Tissue Int 110(1):41-56

### S794
Kelley GA, Kelley KS, Kohrt WM, 2013. Exercise and bone mineral density in men: a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. Bone 53(1):103-111

### S795
EFSA, 2017. Dietary Reference Values for nutrients: Summary report. EFSA Supporting Publications 2017:e15121 (summarises the 2010 carbohydrate/fibre and fat opinions and the 2012 protein opinion)  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121](https://doi.org/10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/supporting/pub/e15121>  
Opened: <https://www.efsa.europa.eu/sites/default/files/2017_09_DRVs_summary_report.pdf>  
Supports: Protein: AR 0.66 and PRI 0.83 g/kg body weight per day for adults, for usual mixed diets; requirement of older adults considered equal to that of adults; twice the PRI considered safe. Carbohydrate RI 45 to 60 E%; total fat RI 20 to 35 E%; saturated fatty acids raise LDL cholesterol dose-dependently, intake as low as possible; trans fatty acids raise total and LDL cholesterol; dietary fibre 25 g per day adequate for normal laxation in adults; data not sufficient to set an upper limit for added sugars.

### S796

Supports: Survey of 286 Australian registered exercise professionals: 88% worked outside their scope of practice by giving individual nutrition advice; authors recommend that nutrition advice taught to exercise professionals be limited to general non-medical nutrition information in line with national guidelines, with onward referral for issues beyond scope.

### S797

### S798
Selenius S, Birgegard A, Forsen Mantilla E, 2022. Preliminary evaluation of the online course 'I Care' targeting eating disorder knowledge and attitudes among sports coaches and fitness instructors. J Eat Disord 10(1):142

Supports: Uncontrolled evaluation in 150 coaches, trainers and fitness instructors: large improvements in confidence and knowledge about warning signs and how to approach someone at risk; the course teaches communicating without commenting on weight, shape and food in negative ways (not tested as an outcome); two authors work for the organisation that developed the course.

### S799
Jespersen SE, Agergaard J, 2021. Evenness of dietary protein distribution is associated with higher muscle mass but not muscle strength or protein turnover in healthy adults: a systematic review. Eur J Nutr 60(6):3185-3202

Supports: 15 studies, healthy adults 20-85: even distribution linked to higher muscle mass in 3 of 7 studies, to strength in 2 of 7 and to protein synthesis in 1 of 6.

### S800
Hudson JL, Kim JE, Paddon-Jones D, Campbell WW, 2017. Within-day protein distribution does not influence body composition responses during weight loss in resistance-training adults who are overweight. Am J Clin Nutr 106(5):1190-1196

### S801
De Leon A, Roemmich JN, Casperson SL, 2024. Daily Dietary Protein Distribution Does Not Influence Changes in Body Composition During Weight Loss in Women of Reproductive Years with Overweight or Obesity: A Randomized Controlled Trial. J Nutr 154(4):1347-1355

Supports: 16 weeks, 43 women with overweight or obesity: even vs evening-skewed protein distribution during weight loss gave no group or group-by-time effects on body composition.

### S802
Moore DR et al., 2015. Protein ingestion to stimulate myofibrillar protein synthesis requires greater relative protein intakes in healthy older versus younger men. J Gerontol A Biol Sci Med Sci 70(1):57-62

### S803
Dobrowolski H, Karczemna A, Wlodarek D, 2020. Nutrition for Female Soccer Players-Recommendations. Medicina (Kaunas) 56(1):28

Supports: Quotes the ACSM 2007 fluid position stand (Sawka 2007): 5-7 mL/kg about 4 h before exercise, a further 3-5 mL/kg about 2 h before if no urine or very dark urine; about 1.5 L per kg body mass lost after exercise; FIFA 1.2-1.5 L per kg.

### S804
Volkert D et al., 2019. ESPEN guideline on clinical nutrition and hydration in geriatrics. Clin Nutr 38(1):10-47  
Type: Guideline · DOI: [10.1016/j.clnu.2018.05.024](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.clnu.2018.05.024) · Files: nutrition

Supports: Recommendation 61 (grade B, BM): older women should be offered at least 1.6 L and older men at least 2.0 L of drinks per day unless a clinical condition requires otherwise; derived from EFSA total water AIs assuming 80% comes from drinks.

### S807
Riou ME et al., 2015. Predictors of Energy Compensation during Exercise Interventions: A Systematic Review. Nutrients 7(5):3677-3704  
Type: Systematic review · DOI: [10.3390/nu7053677](https://doi.org/10.3390/nu7053677) · Files: cardio-health

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=4446773&rettype=xml>  
Supports: 61 exercise-intervention studies, 928 subjects: weight loss usually smaller than expected; mean energy compensation 18% +/- 93%, about 84% in interventions of about 80 weeks; initial fat mass, age and duration explained 48% of variance; candidate reasons include more intake, less expenditure, small dose and poor compliance.

### S808
Herrmann SD et al., 2024. 2024 Adult Compendium of Physical Activities: A third update of the energy costs of human activities. J Sport Health Sci 13(1):6-12

Supports: METs are the ratio of an activity's energy cost to a standard resting metabolic rate of 3.5 mL/kg/min, approximately 1.0 kcal/kg/h.

Supports: 6 adults walking on a treadmill: resting rate 1.08 W/kg, standing 1.25 W/kg; resting and standing rates were large fractions (16-58%) of gross walking cost; subtracting standing instead of resting changed net cost by up to 16.0%; authors suggest net values from subtracting resting metabolic rate.

### S810

Supports: Testing for uncomplicated adults with signs and symptoms of increased risk of moderate to severe OSA: excessive daytime sleepiness plus at least two of habitual loud snoring, witnessed apnea or gasping or choking, or diagnosed hypertension; questionnaires and prediction tools should not be used alone to diagnose OSA.

US Preventive Services Task Force, 2022. Screening for Obstructive Sleep Apnea in Adults: US Preventive Services Task Force Recommendation Statement. JAMA 328(19):1945-1950

### S813
American College of Sports Medicine, Exercise is Medicine, 2024. Being Active During Pregnancy (patient flyer)  
Type: Other · DOI: none · Files: recovery-habits

Supports: Reasons to stop and seek medical advice: bleeding or fluid from the vagina; chest pain; dizziness or faintness not resolving with rest; shortness of breath not resolving with rest; regular painful uterine contractions; calf pain or swelling. Builds to at least 150 min/week of moderate activity. A patient handout, not the ACOG or SOGC/CSEP guideline text.

### S814
Cruz-Jentoft AJ et al., 2019. Sarcopenia: revised European consensus on definition and diagnosis. Age Ageing 48(1):16-31  
Type: Position stand · DOI: [10.1093/ageing/afy169](https://doi.org/10.1093/ageing/afy169) · Files: recovery-habits

Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pmc&id=6322506&rettype=xml>  
Supports: Low strength: grip <27 kg (men), <16 kg (women); chair stand >15 s for five rises; low strength identifies probable sarcopenia. Low muscle quantity ASM <20 kg men, <15 kg women; low performance gait speed <=0.8 m/s, SPPB <=8. Case finding when a person reports falls, weakness, slow walking, difficulty rising from a chair or weight loss.

### S815
Grover M, 2012. Evaluating acutely injured patients for internal derangement of the knee. Am Fam Physician 85(3):247-252  
Type: Narrative review · DOI: none · Files: recovery-habits  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22335264/>  
Opened: <https://www.aafp.org/pubs/afp/issues/2012/0201/p247.html>  
Supports: Clues table (adapted from a New Zealand guideline): ACL tear: pivoting or leaping injury, audible pop, instability, early swelling within one to two hours; meniscal tear: twisting injury, giving way, locking and catching, loss of extension. Internal derangement should be suspected in knee trauma with effusion (SORT C); Ottawa Knee Rule for radiography (SORT A).

### S816

Supports: Meniscal tears can follow acute twisting trauma in people under 40 or be degenerative in people 40 or older with OA; exercise therapy is first-line for degenerative tears; severe traumatic (bucket-handle) tears likely need surgery.

### S819

'REE (males) = 10 x weight (kg) + 6.25 x height (cm) - 5 x age (y) + 5; REE (females) = 10 x weight (kg) + 6.25 x height (cm) - 5 x age (y) - 161'. 'Simplification of this formula and separation by sex did not affect its predictive value'. Added in Phase 2 (2026-10-24) to anchor the coefficients the coach code uses.

Supports: Abstract: 'Ten trials (n = 596) met inclusion criteria. RT+HMB produced modest and borderline significant improvements in handgrip strength (SMD 0.24; 95% CI 0.00-0.48; p = 0.05) and moderate benefits in Short Physical Performance Battery (SPPB) scores (SMD 0.54; 95% CI 0.12-0.95; p = 0.01). ### S824
Bislev LS, Grove-Laugesen D, Rejnmark L. 2021. Vitamin D and Muscle Health: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of Randomized Placebo-Controlled Trials. J Bone Miner Res 36(9):1651-1660  
Type: Meta-analysis · DOI: [10.1002/jbmr.4412](https://doi.org/10.1002/jbmr.4412) · Files: nutrition  
URL: <https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405916/>  
Opened: <https://eutils.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/entrez/eutils/efetch.fcgi?db=pubmed&id=34405916&rettype=abstract&retmode=text>  
Supports: Abstract: 'We included studies in humans (except athletes) on supplementation with vitamin D2 or D3 versus placebo ... In total, 54 randomized controlled trials involving 8747 individuals were included. Vitamin D versus placebo was associated with a significantly longer time spent performing the TUG (MD 0.15 [95% confidence interval (CI) 0.03 to 0.26] seconds, N = 19 studies, I2  = 0%, n = 5223 participants) and a significant lower maximum knee flexion strength (MD -3.3 [-6.63 to -0.03] Newton, N = 12 studies, I2  = 0%, n = 765 participants). ... Other measures of muscle health did not show between-group differences. In subgroup analyses, including studies with low vitamin D levels, effects of vitamin D supplementation did not differ from placebo. Available evidence does not support a beneficial effect of vitamin D supplementation on muscle health.'. Added by research 2026-10-24 (dropped claim D077).

### S825

Supports: Abstract: 'Eligible randomized controlled trials (RCTs) compared the effect of vitamin D supplementation (as monotherapy) with placebo on indices of sarcopenia in older (>50 years) adults. ... Ten RCTs were included in the meta-analysis. Vitamin D supplementation conferred no effect on HGS (MD: -0.07 kg; 95% CI -0.70 to 0.55; I2  = 51%, P = 0.82), TUG (MD: 0.07 s; 95% CI -0.08 to 0.22; I2  = 0%, P = 0.35), ALM (MD: 0.06 kg/m2 ; 95% CI: -0.32 to 0.44; I2  = 73%, P = 0.77), general muscle strength (SMD: -0.01; 95% CI -0.17 to 0.15; I2  = 42%, P = 0.90) ... Vitamin D supplementation did not improve any sarcopenia indices in community-dwelling older adults and may compromise some aspects of physical performance.'. Added by research 2026-10-24 (dropped claim D077).

### S827

Results showed no significant differences in ROM (ES = 0.01, p = 0.88), stiffness (ES = 0.09, p = 0.67), or passive peak torque (ES = -0.30, p = 0.14) between stretching or foam rolling and the other identified activities. ...
